# CHAPTER 12: ARTICLES AND DETERMINERS

## 0. CHAPTER METADATA

**Chapter Number:** 12
**Chapter Title:** Articles and Determiners: Pointing to Nouns
**Unit:** 4 — Relationships Between Words and Ideas
**Primary Concept:** Understanding that articles and determiners are words that introduce and specify a noun.
**Secondary Concepts:** Definite article (the), indefinite articles (a/an), demonstratives (this, that, these, those).
**Prerequisites:** Understanding of nouns (Chapters 3 & 4).
**Estimated Time:** 3-4 hours
**Estimated Pages:** 10-12
**Difficulty:** Medium
**Writing Link:** Using articles and determiners correctly to provide clarity and specificity.
**Speaking/Listening Link:** Asking for and identifying specific objects in conversation.
**Vocabulary Load:** Low

---

## 1. PEDAGOGICAL PURPOSE

**Why is this concept being taught here?**
Students have learned to name, describe, and connect words. Now, they need to learn how to specify which noun they are talking about. Articles and determiners are essential for this, acting as "pointers" to nouns. They are fundamental for clear communication and avoiding ambiguity.

**What should the learner be able to do with it?**
The learner can:
- Define articles and determiners.
- Differentiate between definite ('the') and indefinite ('a'/'an') articles.
- Use 'a' and 'an' correctly based on vowel and consonant sounds.
- Use demonstratives ('this', 'that', 'these', 'those') to point out nouns.

---

## 2. LEARNING OUTCOMES

The learner can:

- [ ] define what an article is.
- [ ] use 'a' and 'an' correctly.
- [ ] understand when to use 'the'.
- [ ] identify demonstratives (this, that, these, those).
- [ ] use articles and demonstratives correctly in sentences.

---

## 3. PREREQUISITE CHECK

### Quick Check

Underline the nouns in the sentences below.

1.  The **boy** is reading a **book**.
2.  I saw an **elephant** at the **zoo**.

---

## 4. LANGUAGE EXPERIENCE (LET'S BEGIN)

Rohan and Maya are helping Ms. Sharma tidy the art supplies shelf.

**Ms. Sharma:** "Rohan, please pass me **a** paintbrush."

**(Rohan looks at a box with many paintbrushes and hands one to her.)**

**Ms. Sharma:** "Thank you. Now, Maya, could you please pass me **the** red bottle of paint?"

**(Maya looks at several bottles of paint—red, blue, and yellow—and picks the specific red one.)**

**Rohan:** "That was easy! But why did you say '**a** paintbrush' to me, and '**the** red bottle' to Maya?"

**Ms. Sharma:** "Great question, Rohan! I said '**a** paintbrush' because I needed any brush from the box, it wasn't a specific one. But I needed one specific bottle of paint—**the** red one. Words like 'a', 'an', and 'the' are called **Articles**. They help us tell if we are talking about something in general or something specific."

**Maya:** "What about when we say '**this** book' or '**that** chair'?"

**Ms. Sharma:** "Those are also special pointing words! We call them **Determiners**. Articles are a type of determiner. Let's learn about them."

---

## 5. CONCEPT DISCOVERY (WHAT ARE ARTICLES?)

**Articles** are words that come before a noun to show whether the noun is general or specific. The three articles are **a, an,** and **the**.

---

## 6. CONCEPT EXPLANATION (INDEFINITE AND DEFINITE ARTICLES)

### A. Indefinite Articles: 'a' and 'an'

We use **a** or **an** when we talk about a person or thing for the first time, or when we mean any one person or thing (not a specific one).

-   I saw **a** lion at the zoo. (Any lion, not a specific one you know.)
-   She wants **an** apple. (Any apple, not a particular one.)

**How to choose between 'a' and 'an'?**

The choice depends on the **sound** of the first letter of the word that comes after the article.

-   Use **a** before words that start with a **consonant sound**.
    -   `a` **b**ook, `a` **c**at, `a` **d**og, `a` **u**niversity (because 'university' starts with a 'y' sound)

-   Use **an** before words that start with a **vowel sound** (a, e, i, o, u).
    -   `an` **a**pple, `an` **e**lephant, `an` **i**gloo, `an` **h**our (because 'hour' starts with an 'ow' sound)

### B. Definite Article: 'the'

We use **the** when we talk about a specific, particular person or thing. We also use it when something has already been mentioned.

-   **The** sun is shining. (There is only one sun.)
-   I saw a lion at the zoo. **The** lion was sleeping. (We are now talking about the specific lion I saw.)
-   Please pass me **the** blue pen. (A specific pen, not just any pen.)

### C. Other Determiners: Demonstratives

Words like **this, that, these,** and **those** are also determiners. They are called **demonstratives** because they "demonstrate" or point out which noun you are talking about.

-   **This** and **These** point to things that are **near**.
    -   **This** book (singular, near)
    -   **These** books (plural, near)

-   **That** and **Those** point to things that are **far**.
    -   **That** tree (singular, far)
    -   **Those** trees (plural, far)

---

## 7. WORKED EXAMPLE

**Ms. Sharma:** "Let's decide which article to use in this sentence."

**Sentence:** `I have ___ new bicycle. ___ bicycle is red.`

**Let's Think Step-by-Step:**

1.  **First blank:** Am I talking about the bicycle for the first time? Yes. Is it a specific bicycle yet? No. So I need an indefinite article ('a' or 'an').
2.  **Choose 'a' or 'an':** The next word is 'new', which starts with a consonant sound ('n'). So, I use **a**.
    -   *Sentence so far:* `I have a new bicycle.`
3.  **Second blank:** Am I now talking about a specific bicycle? Yes, the one I just mentioned. So I need the definite article.
4.  **Choose 'the':** The article is **the**.

**Completed Sentence:** `I have **a** new bicycle. **The** bicycle is red.`

---

## 8. GUIDED PRACTICE (LET'S PRACTISE TOGETHER)

**Activity A: 'a' or 'an'?**

**Ms. Sharma:** "Let's fill in the blanks with 'a' or 'an'. Remember to listen for the sound! I'll do the first one."

1.  I saw ______ eagle flying high.
    -   *Answer:* **an** (because 'eagle' starts with a vowel sound)

**Now, let's do the next ones together.**

2.  He is ______ honest man.
    -   *Answer:*

3.  She is reading ______ book.
    -   *Answer:*

4.  My father is ______ university professor.
    -   *Answer:*

**Activity B: 'a' or 'the'?**

Choose the correct article for each sentence.

1.  Can you see (a / the) moon?
    -   *Answer:* **the** (There is only one moon.)

2.  I bought (a / the) new shirt. (A / The) shirt is blue.
    -   *Answer:*

---

## 9. INDEPENDENT PRACTICE (NOW, YOUR TURN!)

**Activity C: Fill in the Blanks**

Fill in the blanks with **a, an,** or **the**.

1.  My mother is ______ doctor and my father is ______ engineer.
2.  I ate ______ apple for breakfast. ______ apple was juicy.
3.  ______ Earth revolves around ______ Sun.
4.  It took me ______ hour to finish my homework.
5.  It's hot in here. Could you please open ______ window?

**Activity D: This, That, These, Those**

Fill in the blanks with the correct demonstrative.

1.  (Pointing to a book in your hand) ______ book is mine.
2.  (Pointing to some birds far away in the sky) Look at ______ birds!
3.  (Pointing to a pen on the other side of the room) Can you pass me ______ pen?
4.  (Pointing to the shoes you are wearing) I like ______ shoes.

---

## 10. GRAMMAR DETECTIVE (FIND THE MISTAKES!)

**Leo:** (Squawks) "My sentences are perfect!"

1.  I saw **a** elephant at the zoo.
2.  **The** dogs are faithful animals.

**Ms. Sharma:** "Good try, Leo! But there are small mistakes."

**Let's investigate:**

-   In sentence 1, 'elephant' starts with a vowel sound. So, we must use **an**.
    -   *Correct:* I saw **an** elephant at the zoo.

-   In sentence 2, we are talking about dogs in general, not specific dogs. When we make a general statement about a plural noun, we usually don't use 'the'.
    -   *Correct:* **Dogs** are faithful animals.

**Your Turn, Detective!**

Correct the article mistakes in these sentences.

1.  She is a honest girl.
2.  I need to buy the new umbrella. An umbrella should be strong.

---

## 11. WRITING & SPEAKING CONNECTION

**Activity E: I Spy with Articles!**

Play this game with a partner.

1.  **Student A:** Look around the room and choose an object. Say, "I spy **a** [object name]." (e.g., "I spy **a** book.")
2.  **Student B:** Point to a specific object and ask, "Is it **this** book?" or "Is it **that** book over there?"
3.  **Student A:** Reply, "Yes, it is!" or "No, it isn't."
4.  Take turns! This game helps you practice using general and specific words.

**Activity F: Describe the Scene**

Write a short paragraph (3-5 sentences) about a trip to a zoo or a park. Use at least four different determiners we learned about (`a`, `an`, `the`, `this`, `that`, `these`, `those`). Underline the determiners you use.

*Example:*
Yesterday, I went to **a** zoo. I saw **an** elephant first. **The** elephant was huge! I told my mom, "**This** is my favourite animal." I also saw some monkeys. **Those** monkeys were very playful.

---

## 12. LET'S REVIEW (ASSESSMENT)

1.  When do we use 'an'? Give an example.
2.  Fill in the blank: *I have a cat. ______ cat is very fluffy.*
3.  Correct the mistake in this sentence: *Look at these book here.*
4.  Which word would you use to point to something far away from you? (this / that)
5.  What are 'a', 'an', and 'the' called?

---

## 13. LET'S SUMMARISE

In this chapter, we learned about **determiners**, which are words that introduce nouns. **Articles** are the most common type of determiner.

-   They tell us if a noun is **general** or **specific**.

| Determiner Type | Words | Job | Example |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Indefinite Article** | **a / an** | Used for a general noun, or when mentioning it for the first time. | I need **a** pencil. |
| **Definite Article** | **the** | Used for a specific noun that everyone knows, or has been mentioned before. | **The** sun is bright. |
| **Demonstratives (Near)** | **this / these** | Points to nouns that are close to the speaker. | **This** apple. **These** apples. |
| **Demonstratives (Far)** | **that / those** | Points to nouns that are far from the speaker. | **That** house. **Those** houses. |

> **Key Idea:** Using the correct determiner makes your sentences clear and specific.
---

## 14. QUICK CHECK

1. What is an article?
2. When do you use "an" instead of "a"?
3. Fill in: "___ (This/These) shoes are new." (pointing at shoes you're wearing)
4. Correct this: "I saw a elephant."
5. Which article is used for something specific, already mentioned: "a" or "the"?

---

## 15. THINK DEEPER

1. Why do we choose "a" or "an" based on sound rather than spelling (e.g., "an hour" but "a university")?
2. Can "the" ever be used with a plural noun? Give an example.
3. Why might leaving out an article completely sometimes still be correct (e.g., "Dogs are loyal")?

---

## 16. CREATE YOUR OWN

- Write three sentences, each correctly using "a," "an," or "the."
- Create a short "Grammar Detective" sentence of your own with an article mistake for a partner to fix.

---

## 17. SUMMARY

(See Section 13, "Let's Summarise," above for the chapter summary.)

---

## 18. KEY TERMS

| Term | Simple Meaning |
|---|---|
| Article | A word (a, an, the) that introduces a noun |
| Indefinite article | "a" or "an" — used for a general noun |
| Definite article | "the" — used for a specific, known noun |
| Demonstrative | Words (this, that, these, those) that point to a noun |

---

## 19. SELF-CHECK

I can:

- [ ] define what an article is.
- [ ] use "a" and "an" correctly.
- [ ] understand when to use "the."
- [ ] identify demonstratives (this, that, these, those).
- [ ] use articles and demonstratives correctly in sentences.

---

## 20. PRACTICE ZONE

A. **Identify** [R] — Underline the article or demonstrative in five sentences.
B. **Classify** [U] — Sort a list of nouns into "needs a" and "needs an" based on their starting sound.
C. **Complete** [U] — Fill in the correct article or demonstrative.
D. **Correct** [A] — Fix article mistakes (e.g., "a elephant," "the dogs are faithful" as a general statement).
E. **Rearrange/Transform** [A] — Rewrite a sentence changing "this/these" to "that/those" and adjust meaning.
F. **Explain** [AN] — Explain why a given sentence needs "a," "an," or "the."
G. **Apply** [A] — Write a short paragraph describing a shopping trip using at least four articles or demonstratives.
H. **Create** [C] — Build your own "I Spy" game round (as in Section 11) for a partner.

---

## 21. REAL-LIFE TASK

Look at three objects in your home. Write one sentence about each, correctly using "a," "an," or "the," and one sentence using "this," "that," "these," or "those."

---

## 22. TEACHER SUPPORT NOTE

Likely misconception: Students often choose "a" or "an" based on the first letter's spelling rather than its sound (e.g., "a hour" instead of "an hour," or "an university" instead of "a university").

Support strategy: Practise saying the word aloud first, then choosing the article — sound, not spelling, is the rule.

Extension: Briefly introduce "some/any" as determiners for plural and uncountable nouns, building on Chapter 6's quantity adjectives.

Challenge: Ask advanced learners to find and correct all article errors in a short unedited paragraph.

---

## 23. ANSWER KEY DATA

**Section 14 (Quick Check):**
1. A word (a, an, the) that introduces a noun, showing whether it is general or specific.
2. Before a word that starts with a vowel sound.
3. These
4. "I saw an elephant."
5. the

For open-ended items (Sections 15, 16, 21): accept any grammatically correct response using the correct article or demonstrative. Evaluation criteria: correct a/an/the choice based on sound and specificity, sensible original content.

# CHAPTER 13: SUBJECT-VERB AGREEMENT

## 0. CHAPTER METADATA

**Chapter Number:** 13
**Chapter Title:** Subject-Verb Agreement: Making Verbs Match
**Unit:** 5 — Accuracy and Time
**Primary Concept:** Understanding that a verb must agree in number with its subject.
**Secondary Concepts:** Singular/plural subjects, singular/plural verbs, special cases (I/you, collective nouns, words between subject and verb).
**Prerequisites:** Understanding of nouns (Chapters 3 & 4), pronouns (Chapter 5), verbs (Chapter 8).
**Estimated Time:** 4-5 hours
**Estimated Pages:** 12-15
**Difficulty:** Medium to High
**Writing Link:** Ensuring grammatical accuracy in written sentences and paragraphs.
**Speaking/Listening Link:** Speaking grammatically correct sentences.
**Vocabulary Load:** Low

---

## 1. PEDAGOGICAL PURPOSE

**Why is this concept being taught here?**
Subject-verb agreement is a fundamental rule for grammatical accuracy in English. After learning about subjects, nouns, pronouns, and verbs individually, students are now ready to understand how these elements must work together harmoniously. This chapter is crucial for developing correct sentence construction and editing skills, and sits at the start of Unit 5, which focuses on accuracy and time.

**What should the learner be able to do with it?**
The learner can:
- Identify the subject and verb in a sentence.
- Determine if a subject is singular or plural.
- Choose the correct verb form (singular or plural) to match its subject.
- Recognise a few tricky cases, such as words that sit between the subject and the verb.
- Correct errors in subject-verb agreement.

---

## 2. LEARNING OUTCOMES

The learner can:

- [ ] identify the subject and verb in a simple sentence.
- [ ] explain the rule of subject-verb agreement.
- [ ] use singular verbs with singular subjects.
- [ ] use plural verbs with plural subjects.
- [ ] recognise the special agreement pattern for "I" and "you."
- [ ] correct common subject-verb agreement errors, including sentences with words between the subject and the verb.

---

## 3. PREREQUISITE CHECK

### Quick Check

Underline the subject and circle the verb in each sentence.

1. **Rohan** **plays** football.
2. **Maya** and **Leo** **are** friends.

### Remember This

You already know that every sentence needs a subject and a verb (Chapter 1), and that verbs show action or a state of being (Chapter 8). Now you will learn that the subject and the verb must also **agree** with each other in number.

---

## 4. CHAPTER OPENER

Rohan and Maya are talking about their favourite animals, and Ms. Sharma notices something interesting about the way they speak.

**Rohan:** "My favourite animal **is** a lion."

**Maya:** "Oh, I like lions too! But my favourite animals **are** elephants."

**Ms. Sharma:** "Interesting! Rohan, you said 'lion **is**,' and Maya, you said 'elephants **are**.' Why did you use two different words for 'to be'?"

**Rohan:** "Because 'lion' is just one, and 'elephants' are many?"

**Ms. Sharma:** "Exactly, Rohan! You've noticed something very important about how English works. Let's explore it properly."

---

## 5. LANGUAGE EXPERIENCE

**A Class Discussion About Pets**

> **Ms. Sharma:** Let's talk about pets. Rohan, tell me about your dog.
> **Rohan:** My dog **loves** to run in the park. It **barks** at every visitor.
> **Ms. Sharma:** And Maya, what about your cats?
> **Maya:** My cats **sleep** all afternoon. They **wake** up only for dinner!
> **Ms. Sharma:** Notice how "dog" needed "loves" and "barks," but "cats" needed "sleep" and "wake" — without the extra "s." That small change is exactly what we are going to study today.

---

## 6. LOOK AND NOTICE

1. Which sentences above talk about **one** animal? Which verb forms did they use?
2. Which sentences talk about **more than one** animal? Which verb forms did they use?
3. What do you notice about the ending of the verb when the subject is singular?
4. What happens to that same ending when the subject becomes plural?

---

## 7. DISCOVER / EXPLICIT TEACHING

### Explicit explanation

**Subject-Verb Agreement** means that the verb in a sentence must match its subject in number.

- If the subject is **singular** (one), the verb must be **singular**.
- If the subject is **plural** (more than one), the verb must be **plural**.

*Example:*
-   The **cat** **sleeps** all day. (Singular subject "cat" with singular verb "sleeps")
-   The **cats** **sleep** all day. (Plural subject "cats" with plural verb "sleep")

---

## 8. GRAMMAR WORD

## Grammar Word: SUBJECT-VERB AGREEMENT

**Subject-verb agreement** is the rule that a verb must match its subject in number — a singular subject takes a singular verb, and a plural subject takes a plural verb.

### Examples

- The **bird sings**. (singular subject, singular verb)
- The **birds sing**. (plural subject, plural verb)

### Non-examples / Contrast

- "The bird sing." (singular subject with a plural-style verb — does not agree)
- "The birds sings." (plural subject with a singular-style verb — does not agree)

### Quick Check

Does this sentence agree? "The children plays outside." *(No — "children" is plural, so it needs "play," not "plays.")*

---

## 9. CORE EXPLANATION

### A. The Basic Rule

Think of it like this: Singular subjects like to have verbs that end in **-s**. Plural subjects like verbs without the **-s**.

| Subject | Verb Form | Example |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Singular** (one) | Verb + **s** | The **bird** sing**s**. |
| **Plural** (many) | Verb (base form) | The **birds** sing. |

This rule applies to most action verbs in the present tense.

### B. Agreement with 'be', 'have', and 'do'

Some of the most common verbs have special forms for singular and plural subjects.

| Verb | Singular Subject | Plural Subject |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **be** | is, was | are, were |
| **have** | has | have |
| **do** | does | do |

*Examples:*
-   He **is** happy. / They **are** happy.
-   She **has** a new book. / They **have** new books.

### C. Special Subjects: 'I' and 'You'

The pronouns **I** and **you** are special.

-   **I** (which is singular) uses a plural verb form.
    -   I **sing**. (not I sings)
    -   I **have** a pen. (not I has)
-   **You** can be singular or plural, but it always uses a plural verb form.
    -   You **are** my friend.
    -   You **sing** well.

---

## 10. TYPES / CLASSIFICATIONS

### Words Between the Subject and the Verb

Name: Interrupting phrase
Meaning: A group of words (often starting with "of," "with," or "along with") that sits between the subject and the verb but does not change the subject's number.
Example: The **basket** of apples **is** on the table. (The subject is "basket," not "apples," so the verb stays singular.)
Contrast: The **apples** in the basket **are** fresh. (Here "apples" really is the subject, so the verb is plural.)
Quick Check: In "The list of names **is/are** on the board," which subject actually controls the verb — "list" or "names"? *(list, so "is")*

### Collective Nouns as Subjects

Name: Collective noun agreement
Meaning: A collective noun (Chapter 4) names a group but is usually treated as a single, singular unit in Class 5-level writing.
Example: The **team** **is** practising for the match. (The team acts as one unit.)
Contrast: The **players** **are** practising for the match. (Here the plural noun "players" is used instead, so the verb is plural.)
Quick Check: Which verb fits: "Our class **is/are** going on a trip"? *(is — "class" is treated as one group)*

---

## 11. WORKED EXAMPLE

**Ms. Sharma:** "Let's figure out the correct verb for this sentence."

**Sentence:** `Maya (read / reads) a book every day.`

**Let's Think Step-by-Step:**

1.  **Find the subject:** Who or what is doing the action? **Maya**.
2.  **Is the subject singular or plural?** "Maya" is one person, so the subject is **singular**.
3.  **Choose the correct verb form:** For a singular subject, the present tense verb usually ends in **-s**.
4.  **Select the verb:** Between "read" and "reads," the singular form is **reads**.

**Completed Sentence:** `Maya **reads** a book every day.`

**A Trickier Example**

**Sentence:** `The box of pencils (is / are) on the shelf.`

1.  **Find the true subject:** Is it "box" or "pencils"? Read the sentence again — it is really telling us where the *box* is, so the subject is **box**.
2.  **Is "box" singular or plural?** Singular — there is only one box.
3.  **Choose the correct verb:** A singular subject needs "is."

**Completed Sentence:** `The box of pencils **is** on the shelf.`

---

## 12. COMPARE AND NOTICE

**Incorrect:** The bunch of flowers **are** beautiful.
**Correct:** The bunch of flowers **is** beautiful.

What changed? The verb changed from "are" to "is."
Why? The true subject of the sentence is "bunch" (singular), not "flowers." The phrase "of flowers" sits between the subject and the verb but does not change what the subject actually is.

---

## 13. GUIDED PRACTICE

**Activity A: Choose the Correct Verb**

**Ms. Sharma:** "Let's choose the right verb to match the subject. I'll do the first one."

1.  The dog (bark / barks) at the mailman.
    -   *Answer:* **barks** (The subject "dog" is singular.)

**Now, let's do the next ones together.**

2.  The children (play / plays) in the garden.
    -   *Answer:*

3.  She (has / have) a beautiful voice.
    -   *Answer:*

4.  You (is / are) a good student.
    -   *Answer:*

5.  The bowl of oranges (is / are) on the table.
    -   *Answer:*

---

## 14. INDEPENDENT PRACTICE

**Activity B: Fill in the Blanks with 'is' or 'are'**

1.  The sky ______ blue.
2.  Those flowers ______ beautiful.
3.  My brother ______ taller than me.
4.  We ______ going to the park.
5.  This book ______ very interesting.
6.  The group of students ______ ready for the test.

**Activity C: Choose the Correct Verb in Brackets**

1.  A cat (sleep / sleeps) a lot.
2.  Birds (fly / flies) in the sky.
3.  He (do / does) his homework neatly.
4.  I (like / likes) to eat ice cream.
5.  They (watch / watches) a movie every Sunday.
6.  The stack of books (need / needs) to be tidied.

---

## 15. GRAMMAR DETECTIVE

**Leo:** (Squawks) "My sentences are perfect! Everyone listen!"

1.  The stars **shines** brightly at night.
2.  Rohan and Maya **is** good friends.
3.  The pile of toys **are** on the floor.

**Ms. Sharma:** "Good try, Leo! But the subjects and verbs are not agreeing."

**Let's investigate:**

-   In sentence 1, the subject is "stars," which is plural. A plural subject needs a plural verb. The verb "shines" is singular.
    -   *Correct:* The stars **shine** brightly at night.

-   In sentence 2, the subject is "Rohan and Maya." When two subjects are joined by "and," they form a plural subject. The verb "is" is singular.
    -   *Correct:* Rohan and Maya **are** good friends.

-   In sentence 3, the true subject is "pile" (singular), not "toys." The phrase "of toys" sits between the subject and the verb.
    -   *Correct:* The pile of toys **is** on the floor.

**Your Turn, Detective!**

Correct the subject-verb agreement mistakes in these sentences.

1.  My friend live in another city.
2.  The dogs barks when the bell rings.
3.  The set of keys are missing.

---

## 16. COMMON CONFUSION

### WATCH OUT!

Common confusion: Learners often make the verb agree with the **nearest** noun instead of the **true subject**, especially when a phrase like "of the class," "along with his friends," or "of toys" sits between the subject and the verb.

How to check: Cover up the interrupting phrase (the words between the subject and the verb) and read the sentence again. Does it still sound right?

Correct examples:
-   The **basket** (of apples) **is** heavy. → Covering "of apples" gives "The basket is heavy," which sounds right.
-   The **students** (in the class) **are** listening. → Covering "in the class" gives "The students are listening," which sounds right.

---

## 17. LANGUAGE IN REAL LIFE

- **School notice:** "The list of winners **is** posted on the board." (true subject: list)
- **Sports commentary:** "The team **is** playing well this season." (collective noun treated as one unit)
- **Weather report:** "A group of dark clouds **is** moving towards the city." (true subject: group)
- **Everyday conversation:** "My brother and sister **are** coming to visit." (two subjects joined by "and" become plural)

---

## 18. SPEAKING / LISTENING

### Pair Talk

Student A: Make a sentence with a singular subject (e.g., "The girl reads a book.").
Student B: Change the subject to plural and say the sentence aloud, making sure the verb agrees (e.g., "The girls read books.").

Then switch roles and try a sentence that includes a tricky interrupting phrase (e.g., "The box of pencils is heavy" → "The boxes of pencils are heavy.").

---

## 19. WRITING CONNECTION

Write a short paragraph (3-5 sentences) about your family. Describe what each person does or likes. Pay close attention to making your subjects and verbs agree, including at least one sentence with a phrase between the subject and the verb (e.g., "The photos on my desk are of my grandparents"). Underline your verbs.

*Example:*
My family **is** small. My father **works** in an office. My mother **teaches** at a school. The pile of shoes near our door **is** always messy! My brother and I **play** cricket in the evening.

---

## 21. EXACT EDUCATIONAL GRAPHIC

`TYPESET GRAPHIC — NOT GENERATIVE IMAGE`

- Dimensions: full width, 4-row reference chart
- Exact labels: "Subject Type", "Verb Form", "Example"
- Data: Singular subject (verb + -s, "The dog barks."); Plural subject (base form, "The dogs bark."); "I" (base form / am, "I sing. / I am happy."); "You" (base form / are, "You sing. / You are happy.")
- Layout: 4-row chart placed in Section 9, one row per subject type
- Typography: bold "Subject Type" column, plain body text
- Caption: "Subject-verb agreement at a glance."
- Alt text: "A table showing four subject types (singular, plural, I, and you) with their matching verb forms and an example sentence for each."

---

## 22. QUICK CHECK

1. What is the basic rule of subject-verb agreement?
2. Choose the correct verb: *He (has / have) a new bicycle.*
3. Correct the mistake: *The birds sings sweetly.*
4. Fill in the blank with "am," "is," or "are": *I ______ happy to see you.*
5. In "The basket of mangoes ______ (is / are) heavy," which word is the true subject?

---

## 23. THINK DEEPER

1. Why do you think a phrase like "of apples" or "in the class," sitting between the subject and the verb, tricks so many learners into choosing the wrong verb?
2. A collective noun like "team" can sometimes take a plural verb in certain kinds of English (for example, "The team are arguing among themselves," used in some varieties of English to show the team members disagreeing as individuals). Why might a writer choose this, even though "The team is..." is the standard rule taught at this level?
3. Can you think of a sentence where covering up the interrupting phrase (Section 16) would NOT help you find the true subject? Try to create one and discuss it with a partner.

---

## 24. CREATE YOUR OWN

- Write three sentences of your own, each with a phrase between the subject and the verb (like "The bag of apples..." or "The group of children..."), making sure the verb agrees with the true subject.
- Create a short "Grammar Detective" sentence of your own with a subject-verb agreement mistake for a partner to find and correct.

---

## 25. SUMMARY

- The **subject** and **verb** in a sentence must agree in number.
- A **singular subject** (one) needs a **singular verb**.
- A **plural subject** (more than one) needs a **plural verb**.
- "I" and "you" are special — they always use the plural-style verb form.
- A phrase between the subject and the verb (like "of apples") does not change what the true subject is.
- Collective nouns (like "team" or "class") are usually treated as singular at this level.

| Subject Type | Verb Rule (Present Tense) | Example |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Singular** | Add **-s** or **-es** | The dog **barks**. |
| **Plural** | Use the base form | The dogs **bark**. |

**Special Verbs:**

| Subject | be | have |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Singular** | is, was | has |
| **Plural** | are, were | have |
| **I** | am, was | have |
| **You** | are, were | have |

> **Key Idea:** Making subjects and verbs agree is like making sure two friends are working together. It makes your sentences correct and clear!

---

## 26. KEY TERMS

| Term | Simple Meaning |
|---|---|
| Subject-verb agreement | The rule that a verb must match its subject in number |
| Singular | Referring to one person, place, animal, or thing |
| Plural | Referring to more than one person, place, animal, or thing |
| Interrupting phrase | Words that sit between the subject and the verb without changing the true subject |
| Collective noun | A word naming a group, usually treated as singular (e.g., team, class) |

---

## 27. SELF-CHECK

I can:

- [ ] identify the subject and verb in a simple sentence.
- [ ] explain the rule of subject-verb agreement.
- [ ] use singular verbs with singular subjects and plural verbs with plural subjects.
- [ ] use the correct verb forms for "I" and "you."
- [ ] find the true subject even when a phrase sits between the subject and the verb.
- [ ] correct subject-verb agreement mistakes.

---

## 28. PRACTICE ZONE

A. **Identify** [R] — Underline the subject and circle the verb in five sentences.
B. **Classify** [U] — Sort a list of subjects into "singular" and "plural."
C. **Complete** [U] — Fill in the correct verb form to match a given subject.
D. **Correct** [A] — Fix subject-verb agreement errors, including sentences with interrupting phrases.
E. **Rearrange/Transform** [A] — Change a sentence's subject from singular to plural (or plural to singular) and adjust the verb.
F. **Explain** [AN] — Explain why a given verb is correct or incorrect for its subject.
G. **Apply** [A] — Write a short paragraph using correct subject-verb agreement throughout.
H. **Create** [C] — Write your own subject-verb agreement "Grammar Detective" sentence for a partner to fix.

---

## 29. REAL-LIFE TASK

Find three sentences in a book, notice, or app on your phone or a family member's phone (with permission) that use "is/are" or "has/have." Write them down, underline the subject, and check whether the verb agrees correctly.

---

## 30. TEACHER SUPPORT NOTE

Likely misconception: Students very commonly make the verb agree with the noun closest to it rather than the true subject, especially in sentences with an interrupting phrase ("The basket of apples are..."). This is one of the most persistent subject-verb agreement errors even in older learners' writing.

Support strategy: Teach the "cover-up" check explicitly and have students practise it aloud — physically cover the interrupting phrase with a finger or a strip of paper before reading the sentence again.

Extension: Introduce a few more collective nouns (audience, family, committee) and discuss how they are usually treated as singular in Indian English at this level.

Challenge: Ask advanced learners to find or write a sentence where "and" joins two subjects that describe a single idea and are therefore treated as singular (e.g., "Bread and butter is my favourite breakfast," where the phrase names one dish) — a genuine exception useful for class discussion rather than formal testing.

---

## 31. ANSWER KEY DATA

**Section 22 (Quick Check):**
1. A singular subject needs a singular verb; a plural subject needs a plural verb.
2. has
3. The birds **sing** sweetly.
4. am
5. "basket" is the true subject, so the verb is "is."

**Section 13 (Guided Practice A):** 2. play; 3. has; 4. are; 5. is

**Section 14 (Independent Practice B):** 1. is; 2. are; 3. is; 4. are; 5. is; 6. is

**Section 14 (Independent Practice C):** 1. sleeps; 2. fly; 3. does; 4. like; 5. watch; 6. needs

**Section 15 (Grammar Detective, "Your Turn"):** 1. My friend **lives** in another city. 2. The dogs **bark** when the bell rings. 3. The set of keys **is** missing.

For open-ended items (Sections 18, 19, 23, 24, 29): accept any grammatically correct response demonstrating correct subject-verb agreement, including at least one sentence with an interrupting phrase where requested. Evaluation criteria: correct identification of the true subject, correct matching verb form, sensible original content.

---

# CHAPTER 14: TENSE AND TIME

## 0. CHAPTER METADATA

**Chapter Number:** 14
**Chapter Title:** Tense and Time: When Does It Happen?
**Unit:** 5 — Accuracy and Time
**Primary Concept:** Understanding that verbs change form to show when an action happens (present, past, future).
**Secondary Concepts:** Time expressions (yesterday, now, tomorrow), regular verb endings (-s, -ed), common irregular past forms, meaning-based tense choice.
**Prerequisites:** Verbs (Chapter 8), Subject-Verb Agreement (Chapter 13).
**Estimated Time:** 4-5 hours
**Estimated Pages:** 13-16
**Difficulty:** Medium
**Writing Link:** Choosing the correct tense to describe routines, past events, and future plans.
**Speaking/Listening Link:** Talking about daily routines, past holidays, and future plans.
**Vocabulary Load:** Medium (time words, irregular verbs)

---

## 1. PEDAGOGICAL PURPOSE

**Why is this concept being taught here?**
Having mastered subject-verb agreement, learners are ready to explore how verbs also change to show *when* something happens. Tense is one of the most important tools for clear communication, and errors in tense are among the most common mistakes young writers make. This chapter builds a solid foundation in the three main time zones of English before later chapters refine how tenses are used in connected writing.

**What should the learner be able to do with it?**
The learner can:
- Recognise present, past, and future tense verbs.
- Form regular past tense verbs correctly (-ed).
- Recall common irregular past tense verbs.
- Choose the correct tense based on time clues in a sentence.
- Use time expressions correctly with each tense.

---

## 2. LEARNING OUTCOMES

The learner can:

- [ ] identify whether a sentence is in the present, past, or future tense.
- [ ] explain how regular verbs change to show past time.
- [ ] use irregular past tense forms of common verbs correctly.
- [ ] distinguish tense by matching it to time expressions.
- [ ] correct sentences with the wrong tense.

---

## 3. PREREQUISITE CHECK

### Quick Check

Circle the verb in each sentence.

1. Maya **plays** the piano every day.
2. Rohan **finished** his homework.

### Remember This

A verb tells us about an action or a state (Chapter 8). Now we will learn that verbs also carry a clue about *time*.

---

## 4. CHAPTER OPENER

Leo the parrot has been given a diary by Ms. Sharma, and he is very confused about what to write.

**Leo:** "Squawk! Yesterday I **fly** to the window! Tomorrow I **flew** to the window! Today I **will fly**— no wait, that's not right either!"

**Maya:** *(laughing)* "Leo, you've mixed up your times! You need different verb forms for yesterday, today, and tomorrow."

**Rohan:** "That's tense, isn't it? Ms. Sharma told us about that!"

**Ms. Sharma:** "Exactly, Rohan! Let's help Leo write his diary correctly by learning about **tense** — how verbs show time."

---

## 5. LANGUAGE EXPERIENCE

**Leo's Muddled Diary**

> Yesterday: I fly to the window and I eat a seed.
> Today: I sing a song and I sat on my perch.
> Tomorrow: I flew around the room.

Something is wrong in every line!

---

## 6. LOOK AND NOTICE

1. Which time word tells you Leo is talking about the past?
2. Which time word tells you Leo is talking about the future?
3. Does "fly" sound right for something that already happened?
4. Can you spot a verb that is in the wrong time zone?

---

## 7. DISCOVER / EXPLICIT TEACHING

### Explicit explanation

Verbs change their form to show **when** an action happens. This is called **tense**. There are three main time zones:

| Time Zone | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|
| Present | Happening now, or always true, or a habit | Maya **plays** the piano. |
| Past | Already happened | Maya **played** the piano yesterday. |
| Future | Will happen later | Maya **will play** the piano tomorrow. |

---

## 8. GRAMMAR WORD

## Grammar Word: TENSE

**Tense** is the form of a verb that shows the time of an action — present, past, or future.

### Examples

- Present: Rohan **runs** fast.
- Past: Rohan **ran** fast yesterday.
- Future: Rohan **will run** fast tomorrow.

### Non-examples / Contrast

- "Rohan fast run" — no clear tense, not a real sentence.
- Mixing tenses in one sentence without reason: "Rohan runs and jumped." (confusing, usually incorrect)

### Quick Check

Which time zone: "The sun **rises** in the east."?
*(Answer: Present — an always-true fact.)*

---

## 9. CORE EXPLANATION

**Present tense** tells us about now, habits, or facts that are always true.
→ Example: Birds **fly** south in winter.
→ Contrast with past: Birds **flew** south last winter.
→ Example: The class **starts** at nine o'clock.
→ Check: Is this happening regularly? Then it is present tense.

**Past tense** tells us about something that has already finished.
→ Example: We **visited** the zoo last week.
→ Contrast with future: We **will visit** the zoo next week.
→ Example: She **wrote** a letter yesterday.
→ Check: Look for a past time word like *yesterday*, *last week*, *ago*.

**Future tense** tells us about something that has not happened yet.
→ Example: We **will visit** the museum next month.
→ Contrast with present: We **visit** the museum every month.
→ Check: Look for *will*, *shall*, or a future time word like *tomorrow*, *next year*.

---

## 10. TYPES / CLASSIFICATIONS

### Regular Verbs (Past Tense)

Name: Regular past tense
Meaning: Formed by adding **-ed** to the base verb.
Example: walk → walk**ed**, play → play**ed**, want → want**ed**
Contrast: Some verbs double the final consonant: stop → stop**ped**; some drop -e and add -ed: like → lik**ed**.
Quick Check: What is the past tense of "jump"? *(jumped)*

### Irregular Verbs (Past Tense)

Name: Irregular past tense
Meaning: These verbs do **not** follow the -ed rule; they change form completely.
Example: go → **went**, eat → **ate**, see → **saw**, is/are → **was/were**, have → **had**, do → **did**
Contrast: There is no single rule — these must be learned and remembered.
Quick Check: What is the past tense of "go"? *(went, not "goed")*

### Common Irregular Verbs Table

| Present | Past |
|---|---|
| go | went |
| eat | ate |
| see | saw |
| have | had |
| do | did |
| write | wrote |
| run | ran |
| come | came |
| is/am/are | was/were |
| take | took |

---

## 11. WORKED EXAMPLE

**Sentence:** "Yesterday, Maya ___ (go) to the library and ___ (read) two books."

Step 1: Look for a time clue. "Yesterday" tells us this is the past.

Step 2: Check each verb. "Go" is irregular in the past — it becomes "went," not "goed."

Step 3: Check the second verb. "Read" is regular in spelling but pronounced differently; its past form is also "read" (spelled the same, said differently).

Answer: "Yesterday, Maya **went** to the library and **read** two books."

Why: The time word "yesterday" signals past tense, so both verbs must match that time zone.

---

## 12. COMPARE AND NOTICE

**Incorrect:** Tomorrow, Rohan **played** cricket.
**Correct:** Tomorrow, Rohan **will play** cricket.

What changed? The verb changed from past ("played") to future ("will play").
Why? The time word "tomorrow" tells us the action has not happened yet.

---

## 13. GUIDED PRACTICE

**A. Identification**
Underline the verb and state its tense.
1. Maya **sings** in the choir. *(present)*
2. Rohan **jumped** over the puddle. *(past)*
3. We **will travel** to Delhi next month. *(future)*

**B. Matching**
Match the time word to its tense.

| Time word | Tense |
|---|---|
| yesterday | ? |
| now | ? |
| next week | ? |

**C. Completion**
Fill in the correct past tense form.
1. She (eat) ___ breakfast at seven.
2. They (see) ___ a rainbow after the rain.

---

## 14. INDEPENDENT PRACTICE

1. Write one sentence each in the present, past, and future tense about your school day.
2. Change this sentence to the past tense: "I write a story every Sunday."
3. Change this sentence to the future tense: "Leo eats seeds."
4. **Reasoning:** Why do you think irregular verbs like "go" and "went" don't share any letters, while regular verbs like "walk" and "walked" do?

---

## 15. GRAMMAR DETECTIVE

Student sentence:

> "Yesterday I go to my grandmother's house and eated laddoos."

Questions:
1. What is wrong?
2. What should it be?
3. Why?

*(Guidance: "go" should be "went," and "eated" should be "ate" — both are irregular past forms.)*

---

## 16. COMMON CONFUSION

### WATCH OUT!

Common confusion: Learners often add **-ed** to irregular verbs, producing forms like "goed," "eated," or "runned."

How to check: Ask, "Is this one of the special verbs that changes completely?" If unsure, check the irregular verbs table.

Correct examples: went (not goed), ate (not eated), ran (not runned).

---

## 17. LANGUAGE IN REAL LIFE

- **Diary entry:** "Today I **woke up** early and **helped** my mother." (past)
- **School notice:** "The sports day **will be held** next Friday." (future)
- **Classroom routine:** "We **stand** for the morning prayer every day." (present)

---

## 18. SPEAKING / LISTENING

### Pair Talk

Student A: Ask your partner what they did yesterday.
Student B: Answer using past tense verbs. Then ask what your partner will do tomorrow.

---

## 19. WRITING CONNECTION

Write a short diary entry (4-5 sentences) describing:
- one thing you do every day (present),
- one thing you did yesterday (past),
- one thing you will do tomorrow (future).

---

## 20. IMAGE PLACEHOLDER

```text
[IMAGE PLACEHOLDER: IMG-CH14-001]

Purpose: Show a timeline with "Yesterday," "Today," and "Tomorrow" and a simple action (a child flying a kite) in three matching panels.

Educational objective: Help learners visually connect time words to tense.

Placement: After Section 7 (Discover / Explicit Teaching).

Caption: Yesterday, today, and tomorrow — the same action, three different tenses.

Alt text: A three-panel timeline showing a child flying a kite in the past, present, and future.

CHATGPT IMAGE GENERATION PROMPT:

Create an original educational illustration for a Class 5 English grammar textbook. The image should help learners understand the concept of tense as time (past, present, future). Show a child flying a kite in three side-by-side panels labeled "Yesterday," "Today," and "Tomorrow," with subtle visual differences (e.g., sunset for yesterday, midday for today, sunrise/clouds for tomorrow). Use a clean, professional, age-appropriate children's textbook illustration style. The composition should make the intended language concept visually clear. Use original characters and objects. Do not use copyrighted characters, logos or watermarks. Do not place important educational text inside the generated image; essential labels will be typeset separately.

NEGATIVE INSTRUCTIONS:

No watermark.
No logos.
No copyrighted characters.
No unnecessary text.
No distorted objects.
No inappropriate imagery.
No excessive decoration.
```

---

## 21. EXACT EDUCATIONAL GRAPHIC

`TYPESET GRAPHIC — NOT GENERATIVE IMAGE`

- Dimensions: full width, 3-column table
- Exact labels: "Present", "Past", "Future"
- Data: play/played/will play; go/went/will go; eat/ate/will eat; see/saw/will see
- Layout: simple 3-column comparison table, placed in Section 10
- Typography: bold column headers, monospace-free body text
- Caption: "Tense at a glance: three forms of common verbs."
- Alt text: "A table comparing present, past, and future forms of the verbs play, go, eat, and see."

---

## 22. QUICK CHECK

1. What tense is "The dog barks loudly"?
2. Write the past tense of "write."
3. Write the future tense of "she reads."
4. Which time word signals future tense: "ago" or "tomorrow"?
5. Correct this sentence: "Last week we go to the fair."

---

## 23. THINK DEEPER

1. Why do you think English keeps some irregular verbs instead of making all verbs regular?
2. Can a sentence about a habit ("I brush my teeth every morning") ever be past tense? Why or why not?
3. Give a counterexample: a sentence that uses "will" but is not really about the future. *(Hint: promises or requests.)*

---

## 24. CREATE YOUR OWN

- Write three sentences about your weekend: one present, one past, one future.
- Create a "Grammar Detective" sentence of your own with a tense mistake for a partner to fix.

---

## 25. SUMMARY

- Tense shows **when** an action happens: present, past, or future.
- Regular verbs add **-ed** for the past tense.
- Irregular verbs change completely in the past tense and must be memorised.
- Time words like *yesterday*, *now*, and *tomorrow* are strong clues for choosing the correct tense.

---

## 26. KEY TERMS

| Term | Simple Meaning |
|---|---|
| Tense | The verb form that shows time |
| Present tense | Verb form for now or habits |
| Past tense | Verb form for finished actions |
| Future tense | Verb form for actions not yet happened |
| Regular verb | Verb that adds -ed for the past |
| Irregular verb | Verb that changes form completely in the past |

---

## 27. SELF-CHECK

I can:

- [ ] identify present, past, and future tense verbs.
- [ ] explain how regular verbs form the past tense.
- [ ] use irregular past tense verbs correctly.
- [ ] match time expressions to the correct tense.
- [ ] correct a sentence with the wrong tense.

---

## 28. PRACTICE ZONE

A. **Identify** [R] — Underline the verb and name its tense in five sentences.
B. **Classify** [U] — Sort a list of verbs into "Regular" and "Irregular."
C. **Complete** [U] — Fill in blanks with the correct tense of the verb given.
D. **Correct** [A] — Rewrite five sentences with tense errors.
E. **Rearrange/Transform** [A] — Change present tense sentences into past and future.
F. **Explain** [AN] — Explain why "goed" is incorrect.
G. **Apply** [A] — Write a paragraph about a school trip using at least two tenses correctly.
H. **Create** [C] — Write your own three-tense diary entry.

---

## 29. REAL-LIFE TASK

Keep a one-day "Tense Diary." In the morning, write one sentence about what you will do (future). In the evening, write one sentence about what you did (past). Also write one sentence about something you do every day (present).

---

## 30. TEACHER SUPPORT NOTE

Likely misconception: Students often regularise irregular verbs ("goed," "eated," "runned") because the -ed rule is the first one they learn well.

Support strategy: Keep a visible classroom chart of the ten most common irregular verbs; drill orally before written practice.

Extension: Introduce a few additional irregular verbs (bring/brought, buy/bought, catch/caught).

Challenge: Ask advanced learners to sort irregular verbs by pattern (e.g., vowel change only: run/ran; complete change: go/went).

---

## 31. ANSWER KEY DATA

**Section 22 (Quick Check):**
1. Present
2. wrote
3. she will read
4. tomorrow
5. Last week we **went** to the fair.

**Section 13 (Guided Practice B):** yesterday → past; now → present; next week → future

**Section 13 (Guided Practice C):** 1. ate; 2. saw

For open-ended items (Sections 14, 23, 24, 29): accept any grammatically correct, tense-appropriate response. Evaluation criteria: correct tense form, sensible time-word match, original content (not copied from the chapter).

---

# CHAPTER 15: TENSES IN WRITING

## 0. CHAPTER METADATA

**Chapter Number:** 15
**Chapter Title:** Tenses in Writing: Keeping Time Steady
**Unit:** 5 — Accuracy and Time
**Primary Concept:** Using tense consistently and appropriately across connected sentences (routines, narratives, plans), including selected continuous forms.
**Secondary Concepts:** Present continuous ("is/are + -ing") for actions happening now, tense consistency across a passage, avoiding unnecessary tense shifts.
**Prerequisites:** Tense and Time (Chapter 14).
**Estimated Time:** 4-5 hours
**Estimated Pages:** 12-15
**Difficulty:** Medium to High
**Writing Link:** Writing paragraphs and short narratives with consistent, appropriate tense.
**Speaking/Listening Link:** Describing what is happening right now, telling a past story, and stating plans.
**Vocabulary Load:** Low to Medium

---

## 1. PEDAGOGICAL PURPOSE

**Why is this concept being taught here?**
Chapter 14 taught the three basic tenses in isolation. Real writing, however, uses tense across whole passages, and one of the most common errors in young writers' work is switching tense without reason mid-paragraph. This chapter also introduces the present continuous form ("is/are + -ing"), which learners need to describe ongoing action, and teaches them to keep tense steady and appropriate to the purpose of a piece of writing.

**What should the learner be able to do with it?**
The learner can:
- Use the present tense for routines and facts.
- Use the past tense to tell a story that has already happened.
- Use "will" or "going to" for plans and predictions.
- Recognise and form the present continuous for actions happening right now.
- Keep tense consistent across a paragraph and correct unnecessary shifts.

---

## 2. LEARNING OUTCOMES

The learner can:

- [ ] identify the tense used in a passage and explain why it fits the purpose.
- [ ] write a short passage describing a daily routine using present tense.
- [ ] write a short narrative about the past using consistent past tense.
- [ ] use "will" and "going to" to talk about plans and predictions.
- [ ] form and use the present continuous tense correctly.
- [ ] find and correct an unnecessary tense shift in a passage.

---

## 3. PREREQUISITE CHECK

### Quick Check

State the tense of each sentence.

1. Rohan **plays** cricket every Saturday.
2. Maya **visited** her cousin last month.
3. We **will go** to the fair next week.

### Remember This

In Chapter 14 you learned three time zones: present, past, and future. Now you will learn how to use them properly across a whole piece of writing — and meet one new form, the present continuous.

---

## 4. CHAPTER OPENER

Rohan has written about his day for the class newsletter, but Ms. Sharma notices something odd.

**Rohan's Newsletter Entry:** "I wake up at seven. Yesterday I brush my teeth and I will eat breakfast. Then I am going to school and I played with my friends."

**Ms. Sharma:** "Rohan, this is lively writing, but your times are jumping all over the place! One moment you're in the past, then the future, then the present. Let's fix your time-travelling paragraph."

**Maya:** "It's like Leo's diary muddle from before, but in a whole paragraph!"

---

## 5. LANGUAGE EXPERIENCE

**Two Versions of Maya's Weekend**

> **Version A (Muddled):** On Saturday, I woke up late and I eat breakfast. My mother is calling me and I go downstairs. We watched a movie and now we are having lunch.

> **Version B (Steady):** On Saturday, I woke up late and I ate breakfast. My mother called me and I went downstairs. We watched a movie and then we had lunch.

---

## 6. LOOK AND NOTICE

1. Both versions describe the same weekend. Which one feels easier to follow?
2. In Version A, how many different tenses can you find?
3. In Version B, which single tense is used all the way through?
4. Why might switching tense confuse a reader?

---

## 7. DISCOVER / EXPLICIT TEACHING

### Explicit explanation

When we write more than one sentence, we usually keep the **same tense** throughout, unless the meaning truly changes in time. This is called **tense consistency**.

- Writing about a **routine or fact** → stay in the **present tense**.
- Telling a **story about the past** → stay in the **past tense**.
- Describing **plans or predictions** → use **"will"** or **"going to"**.
- Describing something happening **right now** → use the **present continuous** ("is/are" + verb-**ing**).

---

## 8. GRAMMAR WORD

## Grammar Word: TENSE CONSISTENCY

**Tense consistency** means using the same tense throughout a piece of writing (or a clearly marked section of it) so that the timeline stays clear for the reader.

### Examples

- Consistent past: "Leo flew to the window. He landed on the perch. He looked outside."
- Consistent present: "Leo flies to the window every morning. He lands on the perch. He looks outside."

### Non-examples / Contrast

- Inconsistent: "Leo flew to the window. He lands on the perch. He will look outside." (past → present → future, with no reason for the change)

### Quick Check

Is this consistent? "Maya reads a book. She turned the page." *(No — it shifts from present to past without reason.)*

---

## 9. CORE EXPLANATION

**Routines and facts → present tense**
→ Example: "Every morning, the sun **rises** in the east."
→ Contrast: Do not slip into past tense mid-routine: "The sun rises... and it set." (incorrect shift)
→ Check: Is this something that happens regularly or is always true? Use present tense throughout.

**Past narratives → past tense**
→ Example: "Yesterday, Rohan **woke** up early. He **brushed** his teeth and **ate** breakfast."
→ Contrast: Avoid switching to present mid-story: "He woke up... and he eats breakfast." (incorrect shift)
→ Check: Has the whole story already finished? Keep every verb in the past.

**Plans and predictions → "will" or "going to"**
→ Example: "Next week, we **will visit** the museum." / "We **are going to visit** the museum."
→ Contrast: "Will" often expresses a decision made now or a general prediction; "going to" often expresses a plan already decided. Both are acceptable at this level.
→ Check: Is the action still in the future? Use "will" or "going to" consistently.

**Happening right now → present continuous**
→ Example: "Look! Leo **is flying** around the room right now."
→ Contrast with simple present: "Leo **flies** around the room every day" (habit) vs. "Leo **is flying** around the room right now" (happening at this moment).
→ Check: Is the action in progress at this very moment? Use "is/am/are" + verb-**ing**.

---

## 10. TYPES / CLASSIFICATIONS

### Present Continuous — Formation

Name: Present continuous
Meaning: Formed with **am/is/are** + the base verb + **-ing**, used for actions happening right now or temporarily.
Example: I **am reading**. She **is writing**. They **are playing**.
Contrast: Simple present ("I read every day") describes a habit; present continuous ("I am reading now") describes the action in progress.
Quick Check: Complete: "Right now, Rohan ___ (play) football." *(is playing)*

### Will vs. Going To

Name: Future forms
Meaning: Both express future time; "will" often for quick decisions or predictions, "going to" often for plans already made.
Example: "I think it **will rain**." (prediction) vs. "We **are going to visit** Grandma this weekend." (plan)
Contrast: At this level, both are acceptable for general future meaning; the distinction is introduced gently, not tested strictly.
Quick Check: Which sounds more like a plan already decided: "I will go" or "I am going to go"?

---

## 11. WORKED EXAMPLE

**Passage to fix:** "Every day, Maya feeds her fish. Yesterday, she cleans the tank and she will change the water."

Step 1: Identify the intended time frame. The first sentence ("Every day") signals a routine — present tense.

Step 2: Check the second sentence. "Yesterday" signals the past, but the verb "cleans" is present tense — this is an error.

Step 3: Check the third sentence. "She will change" is future, but there is no future time signal — likely meant to continue the past story.

Answer: "Every day, Maya feeds her fish. Yesterday, she **cleaned** the tank and she **changed** the water."

Why: The first sentence stays present because it describes a routine; the second and third sentences must both be past because "yesterday" sets the whole story in the past.

---

## 12. COMPARE AND NOTICE

**Incorrect:** "Right now, Leo eats his seeds and he was singing."
**Correct:** "Right now, Leo **is eating** his seeds and he **is singing**."

What changed? Both verbs became present continuous.
Why? "Right now" signals an action in progress at this very moment, which needs the present continuous form, not simple present or past.

---

## 13. GUIDED PRACTICE

**A. Identification**
Read each passage and name the tense used throughout.
1. "Rohan gets up at six. He brushes his teeth. He eats breakfast." *(present — routine)*
2. "Last Sunday, we visited the zoo. We saw elephants. We had ice cream." *(past — narrative)*

**B. Matching**
Match the situation to the correct tense/form.

| Situation | Tense/Form |
|---|---|
| A daily habit | ? |
| A finished story | ? |
| Something happening this second | ? |
| A future plan | ? |

**C. Completion**
Fill in the correct present continuous form.
1. Look! The dog ___ (bark) at the gate.
2. Right now, Maya ___ (write) a letter.

---

## 14. INDEPENDENT PRACTICE

1. Write a three-sentence passage about your morning routine, using only present tense.
2. Write a three-sentence passage about last weekend, using only past tense.
3. Write two sentences using the present continuous to describe what is happening in your classroom right now.
4. **Reasoning:** Why might a writer deliberately switch from past tense to present tense in a story (for example, to make an exciting moment feel more immediate)? Is this ever acceptable?

---

## 15. GRAMMAR DETECTIVE

Student sentence:

> "Every Sunday, my family goes to the park. Last Sunday, we play badminton and we are having a picnic."

Questions:
1. What is wrong?
2. What should it be?
3. Why?

*(Guidance: The second sentence starts a past narrative with "Last Sunday" but uses present tense forms — it should be "we played badminton and we had a picnic.")*

---

## 16. COMMON CONFUSION

### WATCH OUT!

Common confusion: Learners often mix simple present ("I play") with present continuous ("I am playing") without noticing the difference in meaning, or they drift between tenses mid-paragraph without a time signal to justify the change.

How to check: Ask, "Is this a habit (simple present) or something happening right now (present continuous)?" and "Did my time frame change, or did I just drift?"

Correct examples: "I play football every day" (habit) vs. "I am playing football right now" (in progress).

---

## 17. LANGUAGE IN REAL LIFE

- **Diary routine:** "I wake up, brush my teeth, and walk to school." (present, routine)
- **Story time:** "Once upon a time, a small bird lived in a big tree." (past, narrative)
- **Live commentary:** "The players are running towards the goal!" (present continuous, happening now)
- **Weekend plan note on the fridge:** "We are going to visit Nani this Sunday." (future plan)

---

## 18. SPEAKING / LISTENING

### Listen and Notice

Text/instructions: Ms. Sharma reads aloud two short passages — one about a daily routine, one about last year's holiday.

Response: Students say which tense they heard in each passage and identify one time word that gave it away.

---

## 19. WRITING CONNECTION

Write a short paragraph (5-6 sentences) titled "My Day," including:
- two sentences about your morning routine (present tense),
- two sentences about something that happened yesterday (past tense),
- one sentence about a plan for tomorrow (future).
Keep each part clearly separated so the tense stays consistent within each part.

---

## 21. EXACT EDUCATIONAL GRAPHIC

`TYPESET GRAPHIC — NOT GENERATIVE IMAGE`

- Dimensions: full width, 4-row reference chart

- Data: "I play" / "I played" / "I am playing" / "I will play (going to play)"

- Caption: "Which tense for which purpose?"

---

## 22. QUICK CHECK

1. Which tense should you use to describe something you do every day?
2. Which tense should you use to tell a story that already finished?
3. Form the present continuous of "write" for the subject "she."
4. Correct this: "Yesterday, we go to the market and we buy vegetables."
5. Name one form used for future plans.

---

## 23. THINK DEEPER

1. Why do you think keeping one tense throughout a story helps the reader?
2. Storytellers sometimes switch to present tense in an exciting part of a past story (called the "historic present"). Why might this make the moment feel more exciting, even though it breaks strict consistency?
3. Can a single sentence correctly use two different tenses? Give an example. *(Hint: "I finished my homework before I went to bed" — both past, but consider "I have lived here since I was born" for advanced learners as a discussion point, without formal teaching of present perfect.)*

---

## 24. CREATE YOUR OWN

- Write a four-sentence passage about a class picnic that already happened, keeping tense consistent throughout.
- Create a "Grammar Detective" passage of your own with one tense-consistency mistake for a partner to find and fix.

---

## 25. SUMMARY

- Keep the **same tense** throughout a passage unless the time truly changes.
- Use **present tense** for routines and facts.
- Use **past tense** for stories that have already happened.
- Use **"will"** or **"going to"** for plans and predictions.
- Use the **present continuous** ("is/am/are" + verb-**ing**) for actions happening right now.

---

## 26. KEY TERMS

| Term | Simple Meaning |
|---|---|
| Tense consistency | Using the same tense throughout a passage |
| Present continuous | "is/am/are" + verb-ing, for actions happening now |
| Routine | Something done regularly |
| Narrative | A story, usually told in the past tense |
| Plan/prediction | A statement about the future |

---

## 27. SELF-CHECK

I can:

- [ ] use present tense for routines and facts.
- [ ] use past tense consistently in a narrative.
- [ ] use "will" or "going to" for plans and predictions.
- [ ] form and use the present continuous correctly.
- [ ] find and correct an unnecessary tense shift.

---

## 28. PRACTICE ZONE

A. **Identify** [R] — Name the tense/form used in five short passages.
B. **Classify** [U] — Sort sentences by purpose: routine, narrative, happening now, plan.
C. **Complete** [U] — Fill in the correct present continuous form in given sentences.
D. **Correct** [A] — Rewrite three passages with tense-consistency errors.
E. **Rearrange/Transform** [A] — Rewrite a present-tense routine passage as a past-tense narrative.
F. **Explain** [AN] — Explain why an unmarked tense shift confuses a reader.
G. **Apply** [A] — Write a consistent-tense paragraph about a class event.
H. **Create** [C] — Write your own "My Day" passage using all four situations correctly, clearly separated.

---

## 29. REAL-LIFE TASK

Write two short notes for your family noticeboard: one describing your regular after-school routine (present tense) and one describing your plan for this weekend (future). Read them aloud, keeping each note in a single consistent tense.

---

## 30. TEACHER SUPPORT NOTE

Likely misconception: Students often drift tense mid-paragraph because spoken storytelling naturally mixes tenses; they may not notice the shift in their own writing.

Support strategy: Have students read their own paragraphs aloud and circle every verb, checking that all circled verbs match the intended time frame.

Extension: Introduce "going to" versus "will" as a light discussion point (decided plan vs. spontaneous decision), without formal testing.

Challenge: Ask advanced learners to identify the "historic present" in a simple story excerpt and discuss its effect.

---

## 31. ANSWER KEY DATA

**Section 22 (Quick Check):**
1. Present tense
2. Past tense
3. she is writing
4. Yesterday, we **went** to the market and we **bought** vegetables.
5. "will" (or "going to")

**Section 13 (Guided Practice B):** daily habit → present; finished story → past; happening this second → present continuous; future plan → will/going to

**Section 13 (Guided Practice C):** 1. is barking; 2. is writing

For open-ended items (Sections 14, 19, 23, 24, 29): accept any grammatically correct, tense-consistent response matching the stated situation. Evaluation criteria: correct tense/form throughout, appropriate time-word use, original content.

# CHAPTER 16: PUNCTUATION AND EDITING

## 0. CHAPTER METADATA

**Chapter Number:** 16
**Chapter Title:** Punctuation and Editing: Making Meaning Clear
**Unit:** 5 — Accuracy and Time
**Primary Concept:** Using capital letters, full stops, question marks, exclamation marks, commas, apostrophes, and quotation marks correctly, and applying them while editing a passage.
**Secondary Concepts:** Punctuation as a meaning-making tool, editing checklist, common punctuation errors.
**Prerequisites:** Kinds of Sentences (Chapter 2), Tenses in Writing (Chapter 15).
**Estimated Time:** 5-6 hours
**Estimated Pages:** 14-17
**Difficulty:** Medium to High
**Writing Link:** Editing one's own writing for punctuation accuracy.
**Speaking/Listening Link:** Reading punctuated text aloud with correct pauses and expression.
**Vocabulary Load:** Low

---

## 1. PEDAGOGICAL PURPOSE

**Why is this concept being taught here?**
Learners have now built vocabulary, sentence, and tense accuracy. Punctuation is the final layer of sentence-level accuracy: it tells the reader how to read a sentence and where meaning begins and ends. This chapter consolidates several punctuation marks met individually in earlier chapters and adds new ones (apostrophe, quotation marks), before learners move on to editing whole paragraphs.

**What should the learner be able to do with it?**
The learner can:
- Use capital letters correctly at the start of sentences, names, and titles.
- Use full stops, question marks, and exclamation marks to end sentences appropriately.
- Use commas in lists and after introductory words.
- Use apostrophes for contractions and possession.
- Use quotation marks to show direct speech.
- Edit a short passage to correct punctuation errors.

---

## 2. LEARNING OUTCOMES

The learner can:

- [ ] identify missing or incorrect punctuation in a sentence.
- [ ] explain the purpose of each punctuation mark taught in this chapter.
- [ ] use commas correctly in lists and after introductory words.
- [ ] use apostrophes correctly for contractions and possession.
- [ ] use quotation marks correctly for direct speech.
- [ ] edit a short paragraph to correct multiple punctuation errors.

---

## 3. PREREQUISITE CHECK

### Quick Check

Add the correct end punctuation to each sentence.

1. What is your name___
2. What a wonderful surprise___
3. My name is Maya___

### Remember This

You already know that statements end with a full stop, questions end with a question mark, and exclamations end with an exclamation mark (Chapter 2). Now we will add more punctuation tools to your kit.

---

## 4. CHAPTER OPENER

Ms. Sharma has put an unpunctuated note on the board and asked the class to read it aloud exactly as written.

**The Note:** "rohans mother said dont forget your lunch box maya asked wheres my pencil box"

**Rohan:** *(reading confused)* "It just sounds like one long jumble of words!"

**Ms. Sharma:** "Exactly! Without punctuation, even correct words become confusing. Punctuation marks are like traffic signals for readers — they tell us when to stop, pause, and how to read something."

---

## 5. LANGUAGE EXPERIENCE

**A Note Home (Unpunctuated)**

> dear amma i reached school safely today we have a maths test i will come home by four oclock love maya

**The Same Note (Punctuated)**

> Dear Amma, I reached school safely today. We have a maths test. I will come home by four o'clock. Love, Maya.

---

## 6. LOOK AND NOTICE

1. Which version is easier to read and understand?
2. Where do capital letters appear in the punctuated version?
3. Can you find an apostrophe? What is it doing there?
4. How many separate sentences are hiding inside the unpunctuated note?

---

## 7. DISCOVER / EXPLICIT TEACHING

### Explicit explanation

Punctuation marks show the reader where sentences begin and end, how to read them, and what kind of information is inside them. Each mark has its own job:

| Mark | Job |
|---|---|
| Capital letter | Starts a sentence, name, or title |
| Full stop (.) | Ends a statement |
| Question mark (?) | Ends a question |
| Exclamation mark (!) | Ends a sentence showing strong feeling |
| Comma (,) | Separates items in a list or a pause after an introductory word |
| Apostrophe (') | Shows a missing letter (contraction) or possession |
| Quotation marks (" ") | Shows the exact words someone said |

---

## 8. GRAMMAR WORD

## Grammar Word: PUNCTUATION

**Punctuation** is the set of marks used in writing to separate sentences and parts of sentences, and to make meaning clear.

### Examples

- "Let's eat, Grandma!" (comma saves Grandma from being eaten!)
- "Let's eat Grandma!" (without the comma, it sounds like something very different)

### Non-examples / Contrast

- A sentence with no end punctuation at all: "My favourite colour is blue" (missing full stop — feels incomplete)

### Quick Check

Why does the comma matter so much in "Let's eat, Grandma!"?
*(Answer: It shows we are speaking TO Grandma, not suggesting we eat her.)*

---

## 9. CORE EXPLANATION

**Capital letters**
→ Example: "**M**aya lives in **D**elhi." (sentence start and proper noun)
→ Contrast: "maya lives in delhi." (incorrect — no capitals)
→ Check: Does every sentence start with a capital letter? Are all proper nouns capitalised?

**Commas in lists**
→ Example: "I bought apples, bananas, and mangoes."
→ Contrast: "I bought apples bananas and mangoes." (unclear where one item ends and the next begins)
→ Check: Are there commas between each item in a list of three or more?

**Commas after introductory words**
→ Example: "**However,** Rohan finished his homework late."
→ Contrast: "However Rohan finished his homework late." (missing pause, harder to read)
→ Check: Does a comma follow an introductory word like *however*, *yes*, *well*, or *finally*?

**Apostrophes for contractions**
→ Example: "do not" → "**don't**"; "I am" → "**I'm**"
→ Contrast: "dont" (missing apostrophe — incorrect spelling)
→ Check: Does the apostrophe replace the missing letter(s) exactly?

**Apostrophes for possession**
→ Example: "Maya's book" (the book belongs to Maya)
→ Contrast: "Maya's" vs "Mayas" — without the apostrophe, it looks like a plural, not a possessive.
→ Check: Does the word show ownership? Add 's for singular owners (the girl's bag), and just an apostrophe after -s for regular plural owners (the girls' bags).

**Quotation marks**
→ Example: Rohan said, "**I am ready.**"
→ Contrast: Rohan said I am ready. (unclear whether these are his exact words)
→ Check: Are the speaker's exact words enclosed in quotation marks?

---

## 10. TYPES / CLASSIFICATIONS

### End Punctuation

Name: Full stop, question mark, exclamation mark
Meaning: Marks that close a sentence and show its type.
Example: "It is raining." / "Is it raining?" / "It is raining heavily!"
Contrast: Using the wrong end mark changes the sentence's tone or type entirely.
Quick Check: Which end mark suits: "Can you help me"? *(question mark)*

### Comma Uses

Name: List comma, introductory comma
Meaning: Commas separate items or set off an introductory word/phrase.
Example: "I like tea, coffee, and juice." / "Yes, I will come."
Contrast: A missing comma in a list can merge two items into one confusing phrase.
Quick Check: Add commas: "My pencil case has pens erasers and a sharpener."

### Apostrophe Uses

Name: Contraction apostrophe, possessive apostrophe
Meaning: Shows missing letters, or shows ownership.
Example: "can't" (contraction) vs. "the cat's tail" (possession)
Contrast: "its" (belonging to it) vs. "it's" (it is) — a very common confusion.
Quick Check: Which is correct: "The dog wagged **its**/**it's** tail"? *(its)*

---

## 11. WORKED EXAMPLE

**Sentence to punctuate:** "maya said i cant find my books have you seen them rohan asked"

Step 1: Find where sentences begin — capitalise the first word and any names.

Step 2: Identify direct speech — Maya's and Rohan's exact words need quotation marks.

Step 3: Add the missing apostrophe in "cant" → "can't."

Step 4: Add end punctuation to each sentence, including a question mark for the question.

Answer: **Maya said, "I can't find my books. Have you seen them?" Rohan asked.**

Why: Capital letters mark sentence and name starts, quotation marks show exact spoken words, the apostrophe completes the contraction, and the question mark matches the question.

---

## 12. COMPARE AND NOTICE

**Incorrect:** "the teachers bag was on the table"
**Correct:** "**T**he **teacher's** bag was on the table**.**"

What changed? A capital letter was added at the start, an apostrophe was added to show possession, and a full stop was added at the end.
Why? Each change makes the sentence's meaning and boundaries clear to the reader.

---

## 13. GUIDED PRACTICE

**A. Identification**
Find the punctuation error in each sentence.
1. "wheres my lunch box" *(missing capital, apostrophe, question mark)*
2. "I have pens pencils and erasers" *(missing commas)*

**B. Matching**
Match the mark to its job.

| Mark | Job |
|---|---|
| ! | ? |
| ' | ? |
| " " | ? |

**C. Completion**
Punctuate each sentence correctly.
1. rohan asked wheres my bag
2. i bought milk bread and eggs

---

## 14. INDEPENDENT PRACTICE

1. Rewrite this unpunctuated note correctly: "dear teacher i was absent yesterday because i was unwell i will complete my homework today"
2. Write two sentences using contractions correctly (e.g., don't, isn't).
3. Write one sentence with a possessive apostrophe.
4. **Reasoning:** Why do you think "its" (belonging to it) has no apostrophe, while "it's" (it is) does? What might happen if we mixed them up?

---

## 15. GRAMMAR DETECTIVE

Student sentence:

> "my sister ask can i borrow your book maya said yes you can"

Questions:
1. What is wrong?
2. What should it be?
3. Why?

*(Guidance: Needs capital letters at sentence starts, quotation marks around the spoken words, and correct end punctuation: 'My sister asked, "Can I borrow your book?" Maya said, "Yes, you can."')*

---

## 16. COMMON CONFUSION

### WATCH OUT!

Common confusion: Mixing up **its** (possessive — belonging to it) and **it's** (contraction — it is), and forgetting quotation marks around a speaker's exact words.

How to check: Try replacing "it's" with "it is" in the sentence. If it still makes sense, use "it's." If not, use "its."

Correct examples: "The bird flapped **its** wings." / "**It's** raining today."

---

## 17. LANGUAGE IN REAL LIFE

- **Shopping list:** "Milk, bread, eggs, and butter." (list commas)
- **Text message:** "I'm on my way! Don't wait for me." (contractions, exclamation)
- **Storybook dialogue:** Ms. Sharma said, "Open your books to page ten." (quotation marks)
- **Notice board:** "Whose water bottle is this?" (question mark, possessive pronoun)

---

## 18. SPEAKING / LISTENING

### Pair Talk

Student A: Read an unpunctuated sentence aloud without pausing anywhere.
Student B: Say where you think the punctuation marks should go, and why.

---

## 19. WRITING CONNECTION

Write a short conversation (4-6 lines) between two classmates, using at least one comma in a list, one apostrophe (contraction or possession), and one set of quotation marks for direct speech.

---
