# CHAPTER 21: ACTIVE AND PASSIVE VOICE

## 0. CHAPTER METADATA

**Chapter Number:** 21
**Chapter Title:** Active and Passive Voice: Who Does It, Who Gets It
**Unit:** 7 — Sentence Craft
**Primary Concept:** Understanding that a sentence can show an action either from the doer's point of view (active voice) or the receiver's point of view (passive voice).
**Secondary Concepts:** Identifying the doer and the receiver of an action, simple present/past transformations between active and passive, recognising when passive voice is useful.
**Prerequisites:** Building Better Sentences (Chapter 20), Verbs (Chapter 8), Subject-Verb Agreement (Chapter 13).
**Estimated Time:** 4-5 hours
**Estimated Pages:** 12-14
**Difficulty:** Medium to High
**Writing Link:** Choosing active or passive voice deliberately, depending on what the writer wants to emphasise.
**Speaking/Listening Link:** Recognising both voices in spoken descriptions and news-style reports.
**Vocabulary Load:** Low to Medium

---

## 1. PEDAGOGICAL PURPOSE

**Why is this concept being taught here?**
Having learned to build fuller, more skilful sentences in Chapter 20, learners are ready to see that the same event can be described in two different ways, depending on whether the sentence focuses on the doer or the receiver of the action. This is an early, carefully limited introduction to voice — the goal is recognition and simple transformation, not full mastery of every passive form.

**What should the learner be able to do with it?**
The learner can:
- Identify the doer and the receiver of an action in a sentence.
- Recognise whether a sentence is in the active or passive voice.
- Change a simple active sentence into the passive voice, and back again.
- Notice when passive voice is used in real life (e.g., notices, news headlines).

---

## 2. LEARNING OUTCOMES

The learner can:

- [ ] identify the doer and the receiver of an action in a sentence.
- [ ] explain the difference between active and passive voice.
- [ ] change a simple active sentence (present or past) into the passive voice.
- [ ] change a simple passive sentence back into the active voice.
- [ ] recognise passive voice in real-life notices and reports.

---

## 3. PREREQUISITE CHECK

### Quick Check

Underline the subject and circle the verb in each sentence.

1. **Rohan** `kicked` the ball.
2. **Maya** `wrote` a letter.
3. **The cat** `chased` the mouse.

### Remember This

You already know that every sentence has a subject and a verb (Chapter 1), and that the subject and verb must agree (Chapter 13). Now you will learn that the subject of a sentence does not always have to be the one *doing* the action.

---

## 4. CHAPTER OPENER

Ms. Sharma has written two versions of the same classroom news on the board.

**Version 1:** "Rohan broke the window."
**Version 2:** "The window was broken."

**Rohan:** *(looking worried)* "Wait, both of those are about the same window! But the second one doesn't say who did it!"

**Maya:** "That's true... but it still makes sense, doesn't it? We know a window was broken, even without knowing who broke it."

**Ms. Sharma:** "Exactly, Maya! Sometimes we want to focus on *who* does something, and sometimes we want to focus on *what happens* to something — even if we don't know or don't want to say who did it. Let's explore these two ways of writing a sentence."

---

## 5. LANGUAGE EXPERIENCE

**Two Reports of the Same Event**

> **Report A:** The gardener watered the plants every morning. He also cut the grass on Sundays.

> **Report B:** The plants were watered every morning. The grass was cut on Sundays.

---

## 6. LOOK AND NOTICE

1. In Report A, who is doing the watering and cutting?
2. In Report B, can you still tell what happened to the plants and the grass?
3. Does Report B tell you *who* watered the plants?
4. Which report would you use if you didn't know or didn't need to say who did the work?

---

## 7. DISCOVER / EXPLICIT TEACHING

### Explicit explanation

Every action has two possible "starting points" for a sentence:

- The **doer** — the person or thing that performs the action.
- The **receiver** — the person or thing that the action happens to.

| Voice | Focus | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **Active Voice** | The doer is the subject. | **Rohan** kicked the ball. |
| **Passive Voice** | The receiver is the subject. | The ball **was kicked** by Rohan. |

---

## 8. GRAMMAR WORD

## Grammar Word: ACTIVE VOICE AND PASSIVE VOICE

**Active voice** is when the subject of the sentence performs the action.
**Passive voice** is when the subject of the sentence receives the action.

### Examples

- Active: The chef **cooked** the meal.
- Passive: The meal **was cooked** by the chef.

### Non-examples / Contrast

- "The meal cooked the chef" is not a correct passive of the first sentence — it reverses the actual doer and receiver, which changes the meaning entirely.

### Quick Check

In "The letter was posted by Maya," who is the receiver, and who is the doer?
*(Receiver: the letter. Doer: Maya.)*

---

## 9. CORE EXPLANATION

**Active voice — doer first**
→ Example: **The dog** chased the ball.
→ Contrast: The ball was chased **by the dog**. (same event, passive voice)
→ Check: Is the subject of the sentence the one doing the action? Then it is active.

**Passive voice — receiver first**
→ Example: **The ball** was chased by the dog.
→ Contrast: The dog chased **the ball**. (same event, active voice)
→ Check: Is the subject of the sentence the one the action happens to? Then it is passive.

**Forming the passive (simple present and simple past only)**
→ Example (present): Maya **writes** the letter. → The letter **is written** by Maya.
→ Example (past): Maya **wrote** the letter. → The letter **was written** by Maya.
→ Contrast: The passive always uses a form of "be" (is/am/are/was/were) plus the past participle of the main verb (written, cooked, broken, cleaned).
→ Check: Have I used the correct form of "be" to match the new subject, and the past participle of the verb?

---

## 10. TYPES / CLASSIFICATIONS

### Doer and Receiver

Name: Doer and receiver
Meaning: The doer performs the action; the receiver is affected by the action.
Example: "The baker baked the bread." Doer: the baker. Receiver: the bread.
Contrast: In the passive version, "The bread was baked by the baker," the receiver becomes the subject, but the doer and receiver do not change roles — only their position in the sentence changes.
Quick Check: In "The teacher checked the homework," who is the doer and who is the receiver?

### The "by" Phrase

Name: The "by" phrase
Meaning: In a passive sentence, "by + doer" tells us who performed the action. It can sometimes be left out if the doer is unknown or unimportant.
Example: "The window was broken **by the storm**." / "The window was broken." (doer not mentioned)
Contrast: Active sentences never need a "by" phrase, because the doer is already the subject.
Quick Check: Is a "by" phrase always necessary in a passive sentence? *(No — only when we want to say who did it.)*

---

## 11. WORKED EXAMPLE

**Sentence to transform:** "The children ate the cake." (Change to passive voice.)

Step 1: Identify the doer and the receiver. Doer: the children. Receiver: the cake.

Step 2: Make the receiver the new subject: "The cake..."

Step 3: Choose the correct form of "be" to match the tense (past) and the new subject (singular): "was."

Step 4: Add the past participle of the main verb: "eat" → "eaten."

Step 5: Add "by + doer" if needed: "by the children."

Answer: "**The cake was eaten by the children.**"

Why: The receiver ("the cake") becomes the subject, "was" matches the past tense and singular subject, and "eaten" is the correct past participle of "eat."

---

## 12. COMPARE AND NOTICE

**Active:** The gardener waters the plants every day.
**Passive:** The plants are watered every day (by the gardener).

What changed? The receiver ("the plants") became the subject, and the verb changed from "waters" to "are watered."
Why? The passive version shifts the focus from who does the watering to what happens to the plants — useful when the doer is obvious, unknown, or unimportant.

---

## 13. GUIDED PRACTICE

**A. Identification**
Say whether each sentence is active or passive.
1. Rohan painted the fence. *(active)*
2. The fence was painted by Rohan. *(passive)*
3. The cake was baked by Maya. *(passive)*

**B. Matching**
Match the sentence part to its role.

| Sentence Part | Role |
|---|---|
| The subject of an active sentence | ? |
| The subject of a passive sentence | ? |

**C. Completion**
Change each active sentence into the passive voice.
1. "The cat chased the mouse." → ___
2. "Maya writes a story." → ___

---

## 14. INDEPENDENT PRACTICE

1. Change this active sentence into the passive voice: "The teacher checked our homework."
2. Change this passive sentence into the active voice: "The ball was thrown by Rohan."
3. Write one sentence of your own in the active voice, then rewrite it in the passive voice.
4. **Reasoning:** Why might a school notice say "Homework must be submitted by Friday" instead of naming exactly who must submit it? What does the passive voice let the writer leave out?

---

## 15. GRAMMAR DETECTIVE

Student sentence:

> "The vase was breaking by my little brother yesterday."

Questions:
1. What is wrong?
2. What should it be?
3. Why?

*(Guidance: "was breaking" incorrectly mixes the continuous form with the passive; the correct past participle form is needed: "The vase was broken by my little brother yesterday.")*

---

## 16. COMMON CONFUSION

### WATCH OUT!

Common confusion: Learners often use the wrong form of the main verb in the passive (for example, "was eat" or "was breaking" instead of "was eaten" or "was broken"), because they forget that the passive needs the **past participle**, not the base form or the "-ing" form.

How to check: Ask, "Have I used 'be' (is/am/are/was/were) followed by the special past-participle form of the verb (eaten, broken, written), not the plain verb or the '-ing' form?"

Correct examples: "The window was broken" (not "was break" or "was breaking"). "The letter is written" (not "is write" or "is writing").

---

## 17. LANGUAGE IN REAL LIFE

- **News headline:** "Two students **were awarded** prizes for their science project." (doer not important)
- **Notice board:** "Visitors **are requested** to switch off their phones." (polite, doer understood as "everyone")
- **Recipe:** "The vegetables **are chopped** and then **added** to the pan." (focus on the process, not the cook)
- **Story:** "The treasure **was found** by two brave explorers." (focus on the treasure and the exciting discovery)

---

## 18. SPEAKING / LISTENING

### Pair Talk

Student A: Say a simple active sentence about something that happened at school today.
Student B: Change it into the passive voice and say it aloud. Then switch roles.

---

## 19. WRITING CONNECTION

Write a short "school notice" (3-4 sentences) using the passive voice at least twice, such as announcing a sports day or a book fair. Example starter: "A book fair **will be held** in the school hall next week."

---

## 21. EXACT EDUCATIONAL GRAPHIC

---

## 22. QUICK CHECK

1. In "The bird built a nest," who is the doer?
2. Change this sentence to the passive voice: "The boy closed the door."
3. Change this sentence to the active voice: "The song was sung by Maya."
4. What form of the verb follows "was" or "were" in a passive sentence?
5. Why might a writer choose not to include a "by" phrase in a passive sentence?

---

## 23. THINK DEEPER

1. Why might a news report prefer to say "The bridge was completed" rather than naming every worker who built it?
2. Can you think of a situation where using the passive voice might make a sentence sound less honest or less clear (for example, hiding who is responsible for a mistake)? Discuss with a partner.
3. Is "The sun is shining" an example of the passive voice? Why or why not? *(Hint: think about whether the sun is doing something or receiving something.)*

---

## 24. CREATE YOUR OWN

- Write three active sentences about your school day, then change each one into the passive voice.
- Write a short "Grammar Detective" sentence of your own with a passive-voice mistake for a partner to find and fix.

---

## 25. SUMMARY

- **Active voice** focuses on the doer of an action; the doer is the subject.
- **Passive voice** focuses on the receiver of an action; the receiver is the subject.
- The passive voice is formed with a form of "be" (is/am/are/was/were) plus the past participle of the main verb.
- A "by" phrase can name the doer in a passive sentence, but it can also be left out.
- Passive voice is useful when the doer is unknown, unimportant, or already understood.

---

## 26. KEY TERMS

| Term | Simple Meaning |
|---|---|
| Active voice | The subject performs the action |
| Passive voice | The subject receives the action |
| Doer | The person or thing performing the action |
| Receiver | The person or thing the action happens to |
| Past participle | The special verb form used after "be" in the passive (eaten, broken, written) |

---

## 27. SELF-CHECK

I can:

- [ ] identify the doer and receiver in a sentence.
- [ ] tell whether a sentence is active or passive.
- [ ] change a simple active sentence into the passive voice.
- [ ] change a simple passive sentence into the active voice.
- [ ] recognise passive voice in real-life writing.

---

## 28. PRACTICE ZONE

A. **Identify** [R] — Label five sentences as active or passive.
B. **Classify** [U] — Sort a list of sentences into "active" and "passive."
C. **Complete** [U] — Fill in the missing form of "be" or the past participle in passive sentences.
D. **Correct** [A] — Fix passive-voice verb-form errors in given sentences.
E. **Rearrange/Transform** [A] — Change active sentences to passive and passive sentences to active.
F. **Explain** [AN] — Explain why a writer might choose the passive voice in a given sentence.
G. **Apply** [A] — Write a short notice using the passive voice appropriately.
H. **Create** [C] — Write your own active/passive sentence pairs.

---

## 29. REAL-LIFE TASK

Look for one example of the passive voice on a notice board, in a recipe, or in a news report (with an adult's help). Write it down and explain why the writer may have chosen the passive voice instead of the active voice.

---

## 30. TEACHER SUPPORT NOTE

Likely misconception: Students often use the wrong verb form after "be" (e.g., "was eat," "was breaking") because they confuse the passive with the continuous tense, or they attempt to passivise sentences with no clear receiver (e.g., "It was rained").

Support strategy: Restrict practice at this level to simple, clearly transitive sentences (sentences with an obvious doer and receiver) in the simple present and simple past only. Avoid intransitive verbs (like "rain," "arrive," "sleep") in passive practice, since they cannot be passivised.

Extension: Briefly mention that not every sentence can be made passive (e.g., "The bird sleeps" has no receiver to become the new subject).

Challenge: Ask advanced learners to find a short passage and identify which sentences could be rewritten in the other voice, and whether doing so would change the meaning or emphasis.

---

## 31. ANSWER KEY DATA

**Section 22 (Quick Check):**
1. The bird
2. "The door was closed by the boy."
3. "Maya sang the song."
4. The past participle (e.g., eaten, broken, written, closed)
5. When the doer is unknown, unimportant, or already understood from context.

**Section 13 (Guided Practice B):** subject of active sentence → the doer; subject of passive sentence → the receiver

**Section 13 (Guided Practice C):** 1. "The mouse was chased by the cat." 2. "A story is written by Maya."

For open-ended items (Sections 14, 19, 24, 29): accept any grammatically correct active/passive transformation using the correct past participle and matching "be" form. Evaluation criteria: correct identification of doer/receiver, correct verb form, sensible use of "by" phrase (included or appropriately omitted), original content.

---

# CHAPTER 22: DIRECT AND REPORTED SPEECH

## 0. CHAPTER METADATA

**Chapter Number:** 22
**Chapter Title:** Direct and Reported Speech: Telling What Someone Said
**Unit:** 7 — Sentence Craft
**Primary Concept:** Understanding the difference between a speaker's exact words (direct speech) and a retelling of those words (reported speech), and making simple, correct transformations between them.
**Secondary Concepts:** Quotation marks for direct speech, pronoun changes when reporting, simple tense shift (present → past) when reporting, reporting verbs (said, told).
**Prerequisites:** Punctuation and Editing (Chapter 16), Pronouns (Chapter 5), Tense and Time (Chapter 14).
**Estimated Time:** 4-5 hours
**Estimated Pages:** 12-14
**Difficulty:** High
**Writing Link:** Retelling conversations and events accurately in stories and reports.
**Speaking/Listening Link:** Retelling what someone said in everyday conversation.
**Vocabulary Load:** Low

---

## 1. PEDAGOGICAL PURPOSE

**Why is this concept being taught here?**
Learners already know how to punctuate direct speech (Chapter 16) and how pronouns and tenses work (Chapters 5 and 14). This chapter brings those skills together for a genuinely difficult task: retelling what someone said, rather than quoting their exact words. Full reported speech (with every possible tense backshift) is a demanding skill even for older learners, so this chapter is deliberately narrow — it introduces the idea and the single most common transformation pattern, rather than attempting complete mastery.

**What should the learner be able to do with it?**
The learner can:
- Explain the difference between direct speech and reported speech.
- Identify direct speech (using quotation marks) in a passage.
- Change a simple statement from direct speech to reported speech, adjusting the pronoun and the tense.
- Change a simple reported statement back into direct speech.

---

## 2. LEARNING OUTCOMES

The learner can:

- [ ] explain the difference between direct and reported speech.
- [ ] identify direct speech in a passage, including correct punctuation.
- [ ] change the pronoun correctly when reporting what someone said.
- [ ] change the tense correctly (present → past) when reporting what someone said.
- [ ] change a simple reported statement back into direct speech.

---

## 3. PREREQUISITE CHECK

### Quick Check

Add quotation marks to show the speaker's exact words.

1. Maya said, I am hungry.
2. Rohan asked, Where is my bag?

### Remember This

You already know how to use quotation marks for a speaker's exact words (Chapter 16), and that pronouns like "I" and "he" change depending on who is speaking (Chapter 5). This chapter uses both of these skills together.

---

## 4. CHAPTER OPENER

Maya is telling Ms. Sharma what Rohan said earlier, but she is not using his exact words.

**Rohan (earlier, to Maya):** "I am tired."

**Maya (later, to Ms. Sharma):** "Rohan said that he was tired."

**Ms. Sharma:** "That's a very good way to explain it, Maya! You didn't use Rohan's exact words in quotation marks — you *reported* what he said instead. Notice how 'I am' became 'he was.' Let's find out why."

---

## 5. LANGUAGE EXPERIENCE

**Two Ways of Sharing the Same Words**

> **Direct speech:** Rohan said, "I am hungry."
> **Reported speech:** Rohan said that he was hungry.

> **Direct speech:** Maya said, "I like this book."
> **Reported speech:** Maya said that she liked this book.

---

## 6. LOOK AND NOTICE

1. In the direct speech examples, what marks show the speaker's exact words?
2. In the reported speech examples, are quotation marks used?
3. What happened to the word "I" when the sentence was reported?
4. What happened to the verb ("am" → "was," "like" → "liked")?

---

## 7. DISCOVER / EXPLICIT TEACHING

### Explicit explanation

**Direct speech** shows a speaker's exact words, inside quotation marks.
**Reported speech** tells someone else what was said, without quotation marks, usually with small changes to pronouns and tense.

| Feature | Direct Speech | Reported Speech |
|---|---|---|
| Quotation marks | Yes | No |
| Pronoun | Speaker's own (I, my) | Changed to match the reporter's view (he, she, his, her) |
| Verb tense | Speaker's original tense | Usually shifts one step into the past |
| Example | Rohan said, "**I am** happy." | Rohan said that **he was** happy. |

---

## 8. GRAMMAR WORD

## Grammar Word: DIRECT SPEECH AND REPORTED SPEECH

**Direct speech** is a speaker's exact words, shown inside quotation marks.
**Reported speech** is a retelling of what someone said, without their exact words or quotation marks.

### Examples

- Direct: Maya said, "I am reading a book."
- Reported: Maya said that she was reading a book.

### Non-examples / Contrast

- "Maya said 'I am reading a book'" without a comma or capital letter is direct speech with incorrect punctuation, not reported speech — reported speech has no quotation marks at all.

### Quick Check

Is this direct or reported speech? "Rohan said that he was late." *(Reported speech — no quotation marks, and the pronoun/tense have changed.)*

---

## 9. CORE EXPLANATION

**Changing the pronoun**
→ Example: Rohan said, "**I** am ready." → Rohan said that **he** was ready.
→ Contrast: Maya said, "**I** am ready." → Maya said that **she** was ready.
→ Check: Whose exact words were "I"? Replace "I" with the pronoun that refers to that same speaker (he/she).

**Shifting the tense (present → past)**
→ Example: Rohan said, "I **am** hungry." → Rohan said that he **was** hungry.
→ Example: Maya said, "I **like** stories." → Maya said that she **liked** stories.
→ Contrast: This chapter only covers the shift from simple present (am/is/are, like/likes) to simple past (was/were, liked) — other tense shifts are not covered here.
→ Check: Was the reported sentence originally in the simple present? Then shift it one step into the simple past.

**Using "that" to introduce reported speech**
→ Example: Rohan said **that** he was tired.
→ Contrast: "That" can sometimes be left out in informal speech ("Rohan said he was tired"), but including it makes the sentence structure clearer for learners at this stage.
→ Check: Does the reported sentence flow smoothly with "that" joining the reporting verb to the reported idea?

---

## 10. TYPES / CLASSIFICATIONS

### Reporting Verbs

Name: Reporting verbs
Meaning: The verb used to introduce reported speech, most commonly "said" or "told."
Example: Rohan **said** that he was hungry. / Rohan **told** Maya that he was hungry.
Contrast: "Said" does not need a listener named ("Rohan said that..."), while "told" needs a listener named ("Rohan told Maya that...").
Quick Check: Which reporting verb needs a listener's name: "said" or "told"? *(told)*

### Pronoun Shift Pairs

Name: Common pronoun shifts
Meaning: The most common pronoun changes when moving from direct to reported speech.
Example: I → he/she; my → his/her; we → they
Contrast: Not every pronoun changes — if you are reporting your own earlier words, "I" may stay "I" (e.g., "I said that I was tired").
Quick Check: If Maya says, "I am ready," and you report her words, what pronoun replaces "I"? *(she)*

---

## 11. WORKED EXAMPLE

**Sentence to report:** Rohan said, "I am the fastest runner in the class."

Step 1: Remove the quotation marks and add "that" to join the reporting verb to the idea.

Step 2: Change the pronoun. "I" refers to Rohan, so it becomes "he."

Step 3: Change the tense. "am" (simple present) becomes "was" (simple past).

Answer: **Rohan said that he was the fastest runner in the class.**

Why: The exact words are no longer quoted, the pronoun now correctly refers to Rohan from an outside speaker's point of view, and the tense has shifted one step into the past, as is usual when reporting.

---

## 12. COMPARE AND NOTICE

**Direct:** Maya said, "I **love** my new bicycle."
**Reported:** Maya said that she **loved** her new bicycle.

What changed? "I" became "she," "my" became "her," "love" became "loved," and the quotation marks were removed.
Why? Reported speech retells the idea from someone else's point of view, using the past tense to show that the words were spoken earlier.

---

## 13. GUIDED PRACTICE

**A. Identification**
Say whether each sentence is direct or reported speech.
1. Rohan said, "I am hungry." *(direct)*
2. Rohan said that he was hungry. *(reported)*

**B. Matching**
Match the direct-speech word to its reported-speech form.

| Direct | Reported |
|---|---|
| I | ? |
| my | ? |
| am | ? |

**C. Completion**
Change each sentence from direct to reported speech.
1. Maya said, "I like drawing." → ___
2. Rohan said, "I am tired." → ___

---

## 14. INDEPENDENT PRACTICE

1. Change this direct speech into reported speech: Maya said, "I go to the library every day."
2. Change this reported speech into direct speech: Rohan said that he played football every day.
3. Write one sentence of direct speech about something a friend said to you, then report it.
4. **Reasoning:** Why do you think reported speech usually shifts the tense one step into the past, even if the original statement is still true right now?

---

## 15. GRAMMAR DETECTIVE

Student sentence:

> "Maya said that I am tired."

Questions:
1. What is wrong?
2. What should it be?
3. Why?

*(Guidance: The pronoun and tense were not changed to match the reporter's point of view — it should be "Maya said that she was tired," since "I" refers to Maya, not to the person reporting her words.)*

---

## 16. COMMON CONFUSION

### WATCH OUT!

Common confusion: Learners often forget to change the pronoun, the tense, or both when moving from direct to reported speech, producing sentences like "Rohan said that I am hungry" (should be "he was hungry") when reporting someone else's words.

How to check: Ask, "Whose words am I reporting, and from whose point of view am I now speaking? Does my pronoun match the person being reported, and has my tense shifted into the past?"

Correct examples: Rohan said, "I am happy." → Rohan said that **he was** happy (not "I am happy" or "he is happy").

---

## 17. LANGUAGE IN REAL LIFE

- **Classroom retelling:** Ms. Sharma said that the test was on Friday.
- **Family message:** My mother said that she was busy at work.
- **News report:** The mayor said that the new park was near the school.
- **Story narration:** The old man told the children that he was afraid of the dark.

---

## 18. SPEAKING / LISTENING

### Pair Talk

Student A: Say a simple sentence about yourself using "I am..." or "I like...".
Student B: Report what Student A said, using the correct pronoun and tense. Then switch roles.

---

## 19. WRITING CONNECTION

Write a short paragraph (4-5 sentences) retelling a short conversation you had with a friend or family member this week, using reported speech throughout. Do not use any quotation marks in this paragraph.

---

## 20. IMAGE PLACEHOLDER

```text
[IMAGE PLACEHOLDER: IMG-CH22-001]

Purpose: Show a speech-bubble-to-retelling illustration — one character speaking in a quotation-marked speech bubble, and a second character retelling the same idea in a thought bubble without quotation marks, with an arrow showing the pronoun and tense change.

Educational objective: Help learners visually connect a speaker's exact words to a retelling of those words, with the key changes highlighted.

Placement: After Section 7 (Discover / Explicit Teaching).

Caption: The same words, spoken directly and then retold.

Alt text: A two-part illustration. On the left, a child character speaks with a quotation-marked speech bubble saying "I am happy." On the right, a second child character retells this in a plain thought bubble saying "he was happy," with a small arrow connecting the two bubbles.

CHATGPT IMAGE GENERATION PROMPT:

Create an original educational illustration for a Class 5 English grammar textbook. The image should help learners understand the relationship between direct and reported speech. Show two simple original child characters side by side: the first character has a speech bubble with quotation marks containing simple words; the second character has a plain thought bubble (no quotation marks) retelling the same idea, with a small connecting arrow between the two bubbles. Use a clean, professional, age-appropriate children's textbook illustration style. Use original characters and objects. Do not use copyrighted characters, logos or watermarks. Do not place important educational text inside the generated image; essential labels will be typeset separately.

NEGATIVE INSTRUCTIONS:

No watermark.
No logos.
No copyrighted characters.
No unnecessary text.
No distorted objects.
No inappropriate imagery.
No excessive decoration.
```

---

## 21. EXACT EDUCATIONAL GRAPHIC

`TYPESET GRAPHIC — NOT GENERATIVE IMAGE`

- Dimensions: full width, 3-column change chart
- Exact labels: "Direct Speech", "Reported Speech", "What Changed"
- Data: "I am happy." / "he was happy." / pronoun + tense; "I like books." / "she liked books." / pronoun + tense; "I am ready." / "she was ready." / pronoun + tense
- Layout: 3-column reference chart placed in Section 9
- Typography: bold column headers, plain body text
- Caption: "How direct speech changes into reported speech."
- Alt text: "A table showing three example sentences in direct speech, their reported speech form, and a note on what changed in each."

---

## 22. QUICK CHECK

1. What punctuation mark shows direct speech but not reported speech?
2. Change to reported speech: Rohan said, "I am ready."
3. What does "I" usually change to when reporting someone else's words?
4. What does "am" usually change to in reported speech?
5. Name one reporting verb used to introduce reported speech.

---

## 23. THINK DEEPER

1. Why do you think reported speech does not use quotation marks, even though it is still telling us what someone said?
2. If you are reporting your *own* words from a moment ago, does the pronoun still need to change? Give an example.
3. Can you think of a situation where reporting someone's exact words incorrectly might cause a misunderstanding? Discuss with a partner.

---

## 24. CREATE YOUR OWN

- Write three direct speech sentences using different speakers, then change each one into reported speech.
- Create a short "Grammar Detective" sentence of your own with a pronoun or tense mistake in reported speech for a partner to find and fix.

---

## 25. SUMMARY

- **Direct speech** shows a speaker's exact words inside quotation marks.
- **Reported speech** retells what was said, without quotation marks.
- Pronouns usually change to match the reporter's point of view (I → he/she, my → his/her).
- The tense usually shifts one step into the past (am/is/are → was/were; like/likes → liked).
- Common reporting verbs include "said" and "told."

---

## 26. KEY TERMS

| Term | Simple Meaning |
|---|---|
| Direct speech | A speaker's exact words, shown in quotation marks |
| Reported speech | A retelling of what someone said, without quotation marks |
| Reporting verb | The verb that introduces reported speech (said, told) |
| Tense shift | The change from present to past tense when reporting |
| Pronoun shift | The change of pronoun to match the reporter's point of view |

---

## 27. SELF-CHECK

I can:

- [ ] explain the difference between direct and reported speech.
- [ ] identify direct speech in a passage.
- [ ] change the pronoun correctly when reporting speech.
- [ ] change the tense correctly when reporting speech.
- [ ] change reported speech back into direct speech.

---

## 28. PRACTICE ZONE

A. **Identify** [R] — Label five sentences as direct or reported speech.
B. **Classify** [U] — Sort a list of sentences into "direct" and "reported."
C. **Complete** [U] — Fill in the correct pronoun or verb form in reported speech.
D. **Correct** [A] — Fix pronoun or tense errors in reported speech.
E. **Rearrange/Transform** [A] — Change direct speech to reported speech and back again.
F. **Explain** [AN] — Explain why a pronoun or tense needed to change in a given example.
G. **Apply** [A] — Write a short retelling of a conversation using reported speech.
H. **Create** [C] — Write your own direct/reported speech sentence pairs.

---

## 29. REAL-LIFE TASK

Think of something a family member said to you today. Write their exact words as direct speech with correct punctuation, then write the same idea as reported speech.

---

## 30. TEACHER SUPPORT NOTE

Likely misconception: Students often change either the pronoun or the tense but not both, or attempt to apply reported speech to questions and commands before they are ready, since this chapter deliberately limits scope to statements only.

Editorial note: All examples and exercises in this chapter must use only the simple present (am/is/are, like/likes) to simple past (was/were, liked) shift. Do not introduce continuous forms ("is going" → "was going") or modal backshifts ("will" → "would") anywhere in this chapter, including in "Language in Real Life" or practice items — these are out of scope and were removed during review (see Log.md DEC-034).

Support strategy: Practise the pronoun change and the tense change as two separate, explicit steps before combining them, using the same sentence each time so learners can isolate what changes.

Extension: Briefly mention that reporting questions and commands ("she asked if...", "he told me to...") uses different patterns, to be covered in a later grade.

Challenge: Ask advanced learners to report a short exchange between two speakers, correctly tracking two different pronoun shifts in the same passage.

---

## 31. ANSWER KEY DATA

**Section 22 (Quick Check):**
1. Quotation marks
2. "Rohan said that he was ready."
3. he or she
4. was
5. said (or told)

**Section 13 (Guided Practice B):** I → he/she; my → his/her; am → was

**Section 13 (Guided Practice C):** 1. "Maya said that she liked drawing." 2. "Rohan said that he was tired."

For open-ended items (Sections 14, 19, 24, 29): accept any grammatically correct direct-to-reported (or reported-to-direct) transformation with correctly matched pronoun and simple past tense. Evaluation criteria: correct pronoun shift, correct tense shift, correct punctuation (quotation marks present only in direct speech), original content.

---

# CHAPTER 23: CONVERSATION AND EVERYDAY ENGLISH

## 0. CHAPTER METADATA

**Chapter Number:** 23
**Chapter Title:** Conversation and Everyday English: Talking with Confidence
**Unit:** 8 — Communication and Writing
**Primary Concept:** Using appropriate spoken English for greetings, requests, questions, responses, and clarification in everyday and classroom situations.
**Secondary Concepts:** Polite request forms, asking for clarification, appropriate register for different situations, turn-taking in conversation.
**Prerequisites:** Kinds of Sentences (Chapter 2), Direct and Reported Speech (Chapter 22).
**Estimated Time:** 3-4 hours
**Estimated Pages:** 10-12
**Difficulty:** Easy to Medium
**Writing Link:** Writing natural-sounding dialogue.
**Speaking/Listening Link:** This entire chapter is speaking/listening focused — greetings, requests, questions, responses, and role play.
**Vocabulary Load:** Medium

---

## 1. PEDAGOGICAL PURPOSE

**Why is this concept being taught here?**
Learners have now built a strong grammatical foundation and can construct accurate sentences of many kinds. This chapter turns that knowledge outward, into real spoken communication — the everyday language of greeting people, making requests, asking questions, responding politely, and asking for clarification when something is unclear. It is less about a single grammar rule and more about using the grammar already learned appropriately and politely.

**What should the learner be able to do with it?**
The learner can:
- Use appropriate greetings for different times of day and situations.
- Make polite requests using suitable phrases.
- Ask questions to get information or clarification.
- Respond appropriately to greetings, requests, and questions.
- Take part in a short role play using natural, polite classroom English.

---

## 2. LEARNING OUTCOMES

The learner can:

- [ ] use appropriate greetings for different situations.
- [ ] make a polite request using suitable phrases (e.g., "Could you...", "May I...").
- [ ] ask a question to get information or ask for clarification.
- [ ] respond appropriately and politely in a conversation.
- [ ] take part in a short, natural-sounding role play.

---

## 3. PREREQUISITE CHECK

### Quick Check

Which of these is a polite request, and which is a command?

1. Pass the salt.
2. Could you please pass the salt?

### Remember This

You already know how to form questions and commands (Chapter 2) and how to report what someone said (Chapter 22). Now you will use these skills to communicate politely and clearly in everyday situations.

---

## 4. CHAPTER OPENER

Rohan is practising for a class role play, but Ms. Sharma notices something.

**Rohan:** *(to an imaginary shopkeeper)* "Give me a pencil!"

**Ms. Sharma:** "Rohan, that would work at home with your little brother, perhaps! But if you walked into a shop and said that, how do you think the shopkeeper might feel?"

**Rohan:** *(laughing)* "Oh... maybe a bit surprised! I should ask more politely."

**Maya:** "You could say, 'Could I have a pencil, please?'"

**Ms. Sharma:** "Exactly, Maya! The words we choose change how our message sounds, even if the basic idea is the same. Let's explore how to speak politely and clearly in different everyday situations."

---

## 5. LANGUAGE EXPERIENCE

**Two Ways of Asking**

> **Version A:** "Give me that book."
> **Version B:** "Could I please borrow that book?"

**A Short Conversation**

> **Maya:** Good morning! How are you today?
> **Rohan:** I'm well, thank you. And you?
> **Maya:** I'm fine, thanks. Sorry, could you repeat that? I didn't quite hear you.
> **Rohan:** Of course — I said, "How are you?"

---

## 6. LOOK AND NOTICE

1. Which version of the request (A or B) sounds more polite? Why?
2. In the conversation, how does Maya greet Rohan?
3. What phrase does Maya use when she doesn't understand something?
4. How does Rohan respond when Maya asks him to repeat himself?

---

## 7. DISCOVER / EXPLICIT TEACHING

### Explicit explanation

Everyday spoken English uses certain useful phrases for common purposes:

| Purpose | Useful Phrases |
|---|---|
| Greeting | "Good morning/afternoon/evening," "Hello," "Hi, how are you?" |
| Making a polite request | "Could you please...", "May I...", "Would you mind..." |
| Asking a question | "What...", "Where...", "Why...", "Can you tell me...?" |
| Responding politely | "Yes, of course," "I'm sorry, but...", "Thank you," "You're welcome." |
| Asking for clarification | "Sorry, could you repeat that?", "What do you mean?", "Could you explain that again?" |

---

## 8. GRAMMAR WORD

## Grammar Word: REGISTER

**Register** means choosing words and a tone that suit the situation and the person you are speaking to — for example, speaking more formally to a stranger or a teacher, and more casually to a close friend.

### Examples

- To a teacher: "Excuse me, may I ask a question?"
- To a close friend: "Hey, quick question!"

### Non-examples / Contrast

- Using very casual language with a shopkeeper you have never met ("Oi, give me that!") does not match the situation and can sound rude, even if it is not meant that way.

### Quick Check

Which sounds more appropriate when speaking to your school principal: "What's up?" or "Good morning, may I speak with you?"

---

## 9. CORE EXPLANATION

**Greeting appropriately**
→ Example: "Good morning, Ms. Sharma." (formal, to a teacher)
→ Contrast: "Hi!" (casual, to a friend)
→ Check: Does my greeting match the time of day and the person I am speaking to?

**Making polite requests**
→ Example: "Could you please pass the ruler?"
→ Contrast: "Give me the ruler." (blunt, less polite)
→ Check: Have I used a polite phrase like "could," "please," or "may I," instead of only a command?

**Asking for clarification**
→ Example: "Sorry, could you say that again, please?"
→ Contrast: Staying silent and guessing what was said can lead to misunderstandings.
→ Check: If I did not understand something, have I asked politely instead of guessing?

**Responding appropriately**
→ Example: "Thank you for asking! I'm doing well." (response to a greeting)
→ Contrast: Ignoring a greeting or question is impolite, even if unintentional.
→ Check: Have I responded to what the other person actually said or asked?

---

## 10. TYPES / CLASSIFICATIONS

### Greetings by Time of Day

Name: Time-based greetings
Meaning: Different greetings suit different times of day.
Example: "Good morning" (before noon), "Good afternoon" (noon to evening), "Good evening" (evening onward)
Contrast: "Good night" is used only when parting or going to bed, not as a greeting on meeting someone.
Quick Check: Which greeting would you use at 4 PM? *("Good afternoon")*

### Polite Request Phrases

Name: Request starters
Meaning: Phrases that soften a request and make it sound polite rather than like an order.
Example: "Could you...", "Would you mind...", "May I..."
Contrast: A direct command ("Close the door.") is not impolite in every situation, but adding a request starter often makes it sound more courteous ("Could you close the door, please?").
Quick Check: Which sounds more polite: "Open the window" or "Could you open the window, please?"

---

## 11. WORKED EXAMPLE

**Situation:** You want to ask your teacher if you can leave the classroom to get some water.

Step 1: Think about who you are speaking to — a teacher, so a polite, slightly formal register is appropriate.

Step 2: Choose a polite request phrase — "May I..." or "Could I..." both work well for asking permission.

Step 3: State the request clearly and add "please" for extra politeness.

Answer: "**Excuse me, may I please go and get some water?**"

Why: "Excuse me" politely gets the teacher's attention, "may I" is an appropriate way to ask permission from an adult, and "please" adds courtesy.

---

## 12. COMPARE AND NOTICE

**Less polite:** "I don't get it. Say it again."
**More polite:** "I'm sorry, I don't quite understand. Could you please explain that again?"

What changed? The blunt statement was replaced with an apology, a softer way of admitting confusion, and a polite request phrase.
Why? The more polite version is just as clear, but it is more likely to be well received and shows respect for the listener.

---

## 13. GUIDED PRACTICE

**A. Identification**
Say whether each phrase is a greeting, a request, or a way of asking for clarification.
1. "Good morning!" *(greeting)*
2. "Could you please pass the book?" *(request)*
3. "Sorry, could you repeat that?" *(asking for clarification)*

**B. Matching**
Match the situation to the appropriate greeting.

| Situation | Greeting |
|---|---|
| Meeting your teacher at 8 AM | ? |
| Meeting a friend at 5 PM | ? |
| Saying goodbye at bedtime | ? |

**C. Completion**
Make each blunt sentence more polite.
1. "Give me a pencil." → ___
2. "Tell me the time." → ___

---

## 14. INDEPENDENT PRACTICE

1. Write a short greeting you would use to meet your school principal for the first time.
2. Write a polite request asking a classmate to lend you their eraser.
3. Write a sentence you could use if you did not hear what someone said clearly.
4. **Reasoning:** Why might the same request sound polite to one person and rude to another, depending on how it is said?

---

## 15. GRAMMAR DETECTIVE

Student sentence:

> "Give me your notebook now, I need it."

Questions:
1. What is wrong with how this request is made?
2. How could it be said more politely?
3. Why does this matter in everyday conversation?

*(Guidance: The request is blunt and lacks any polite phrase; a better version might be "Could I please borrow your notebook? I need it for a moment." Politeness helps the listener feel respected, making cooperation more likely.)*

---

## 16. COMMON CONFUSION

### WATCH OUT!

Common confusion: Learners sometimes think that being polite means using very long or complicated sentences, when in fact even a short phrase can be polite if it includes the right words ("please," "could," "may I").

How to check: Ask, "Have I included at least one polite word or phrase, even if my sentence is short?"

Correct examples: "Could I have a pencil, please?" is both short and polite — length is not what makes a sentence courteous.

---

## 17. LANGUAGE IN REAL LIFE

- **At school:** "Excuse me, may I ask a question?"
- **At a shop:** "Could I have a bottle of water, please?"
- **On the phone:** "Hello, this is Maya speaking. May I speak with Rohan, please?"
- **Asking for help:** "I'm sorry to bother you, but could you help me with this?"

---

## 18. SPEAKING / LISTENING

### Role Play: At the School Office

Work with a partner. One person is a student, and the other is the school office assistant.

**Student:** "Good morning! May I please have a new exercise book?"
**Office Assistant:** "Good morning! Of course, here you are."
**Student:** "Thank you very much."
**Office Assistant:** "You're welcome."

Take turns being the student and the office assistant. Try changing the situation (asking for a form, asking for directions to the library) and adjust your polite phrases accordingly.

---

## 19. WRITING CONNECTION

Write a short dialogue (6-8 lines) between two characters in a shop, a school office, or a library. Include at least one greeting, one polite request, and one polite response.

---

## 21. EXACT EDUCATIONAL GRAPHIC

`TYPESET GRAPHIC — NOT GENERATIVE IMAGE`

- Exact labels: "Purpose", "Useful Phrases"

---

## 22. QUICK CHECK

1. What greeting would you use to meet someone at 10 AM?
2. Change this into a polite request: "Give me a pen."
3. What could you say if you did not hear someone clearly?
4. Give one example of a polite response to "Thank you."
5. Why is register (choosing appropriate words) important in conversation?

---

## 23. THINK DEEPER

1. Why might the same words sound polite in one situation but rude in another (for example, speaking to a friend versus a stranger)?
2. Can silence ever be a polite response? Think of a situation where staying quiet might be the most respectful choice.
3. Why do you think many languages, not just English, have special polite phrases for requests and greetings?

---

## 24. CREATE YOUR OWN

- Write a short conversation (4-6 lines) between yourself and a new student, including a greeting and a polite question.
- Create your own "Grammar Detective" example of a blunt request, and rewrite it politely.

---

## 25. SUMMARY

- Choose greetings that match the time of day and the situation.
- Use polite phrases like "could you," "may I," and "please" for requests.
- Ask questions clearly, and ask for clarification politely if you do not understand.
- Respond appropriately to greetings, requests, and questions.
- **Register** means adjusting your language to suit the person and situation.

---

## 26. KEY TERMS

| Term | Simple Meaning |
|---|---|
| Register | Choosing words and tone to suit the situation |
| Greeting | A polite way of meeting someone |
| Polite request | Asking for something in a courteous way |
| Clarification | Asking someone to explain or repeat something unclear |
| Role play | Acting out a conversation to practise real-life language |

---

## 27. SELF-CHECK

I can:

- [ ] use appropriate greetings for different situations.
- [ ] make a polite request.
- [ ] ask a question to get information or clarification.
- [ ] respond appropriately in a conversation.
- [ ] take part in a short role play using natural, polite English.

---

## 28. PRACTICE ZONE

A. **Identify** [R] — Label phrases as greeting, request, or clarification.
B. **Classify** [U] — Sort phrases by the situation they best suit.
C. **Complete** [U] — Turn blunt sentences into polite ones.
D. **Correct** [A] — Rewrite an impolite exchange more courteously.
E. **Rearrange/Transform** [A] — Change a command into a polite request.
F. **Explain** [AN] — Explain why a certain phrase suits (or does not suit) a given situation.
G. **Apply** [A] — Write a short polite dialogue for a real-life situation.
H. **Create** [C] — Perform an original role play with a partner.

---

## 29. REAL-LIFE TASK

This week, notice one polite phrase an adult uses when speaking to you or to someone else (at home, school, or a shop). Write it down and explain what makes it polite.

---

## 30. TEACHER SUPPORT NOTE

Likely misconception: Students may believe politeness only matters in formal writing, not in spoken conversation, or may over-formalise their speech with friends when a casual register is perfectly appropriate.

Support strategy: Use paired role play extensively, and discuss as a class how the same message can be delivered in different registers depending on the listener.

Extension: Introduce telephone etiquette as a specific real-life register (formal greeting, clear identification, polite closing).

Challenge: Ask advanced learners to write the same short conversation twice — once for a close friend and once for a stranger — and discuss what changed.

---

## 31. ANSWER KEY DATA

**Section 22 (Quick Check):**
1. "Good morning"
2. "Could you please give me a pen?" (or similar polite phrasing)
3. "Sorry, could you repeat that, please?" (or similar)
4. "You're welcome" (or "No problem," "Anytime")
5. Because using the appropriate words and tone for the situation and listener helps communication go smoothly and shows respect.

**Section 13 (Guided Practice B):** meeting teacher at 8 AM → "Good morning"; meeting friend at 5 PM → "Good afternoon" or "Hi"; saying goodbye at bedtime → "Good night"

**Section 13 (Guided Practice C):** 1. "Could I please have a pencil?" 2. "Could you please tell me the time?"

For open-ended items (Sections 14, 19, 24, 29): accept any grammatically correct, appropriately polite response matching the situation. Evaluation criteria: suitable register, presence of polite phrasing where required, natural-sounding dialogue, original content.

# CHAPTER 24: PARAGRAPH AND DESCRIPTIVE WRITING

## 0. CHAPTER METADATA

**Chapter Number:** 24
**Chapter Title:** Paragraph and Descriptive Writing: Painting Pictures with Words
**Unit:** 8 — Communication and Writing
**Primary Concept:** Building a well-organised paragraph around a topic sentence and supporting details, and using descriptive detail and sensory language to write vividly.
**Secondary Concepts:** Topic sentence, supporting details, logical sequence, sensory description, editing a paragraph for clarity and order.
**Prerequisites:** Building Better Sentences (Chapter 20), Conversation and Everyday English (Chapter 23).
**Estimated Time:** 5-6 hours
**Estimated Pages:** 14-17
**Difficulty:** Medium to High
**Writing Link:** This entire chapter is writing-focused — planning, drafting, and revising a paragraph.
**Speaking/Listening Link:** Describing a scene aloud before writing it; listening for missing or out-of-order details.
**Vocabulary Load:** Medium

---

## 1. PEDAGOGICAL PURPOSE

**Why is this concept being taught here?**
Learners have already built accuracy at the word and sentence level (Units 2-5), expanded their vocabulary (Unit 6), and learned to craft skilful, connected sentences (Unit 7). This chapter takes the next natural step: organising several connected sentences into a single, purposeful **paragraph**. It also introduces descriptive writing, teaching learners to choose vivid, specific, sensory detail rather than vague description — a skill that will support every writing task in the chapters that follow (letters, notices, and creative composition).

**What should the learner be able to do with it?**
The learner can:
- Identify the topic sentence of a paragraph.
- Write a clear topic sentence for a given subject.
- Add supporting details that relate directly to the topic sentence.
- Arrange sentences within a paragraph in a logical order.
- Use sensory and specific descriptive detail instead of vague description.
- Edit a paragraph for unity (removing sentences that do not belong) and order.

---

## 2. LEARNING OUTCOMES

The learner can:

- [ ] explain what a paragraph is and why it needs a topic sentence.
- [ ] identify the topic sentence and supporting details in a given paragraph.
- [ ] write a topic sentence that clearly introduces a subject.
- [ ] add at least three relevant supporting details to a topic sentence.
- [ ] arrange sentences in a paragraph in a clear, logical sequence.
- [ ] use sensory details (sight, sound, smell, touch, taste) to make description vivid.
- [ ] edit a paragraph to remove an unrelated sentence and fix ordering.

---

## 3. PREREQUISITE CHECK

### Quick Check

Combine this pair of short sentences into one smoother sentence, using a joining word (Chapter 20).

1. The rain stopped. We went outside to play.
   ____________________________________

### Remember This

You already know how to build a single skilful sentence (Chapter 20). Now you will learn how several sentences work together to form one clear, organised **paragraph**.

---

## 4. CHAPTER OPENER

Ms. Sharma has asked the class to write about their favourite place, and she reads out two very different attempts.

**Rohan's Paragraph:** "My favourite place is the beach. I like sand. My uncle has a red car. The waves are big. I eat mangoes in summer. The beach is fun."

**Maya's Paragraph:** "My favourite place is the beach near my grandmother's house. The soft, warm sand squeezes between my toes as I walk towards the water. Tall waves crash and hiss, then pull back with a hush. Seagulls cry overhead, circling for scraps of food. I could stay there all day, just listening to the sea."

**Ms. Sharma:** "Rohan, your paragraph has some lovely ideas hiding inside it, but one sentence doesn't belong at all — can you spot it? Maya, your paragraph stays on one clear idea from start to finish, and I can almost feel the sand and hear the waves! Let's find out what makes a paragraph like Maya's work so well."

---

## 5. LANGUAGE EXPERIENCE

**A Paragraph About the School Garden**

> The school garden is a peaceful corner behind the main building. Bright red and yellow marigolds line the narrow gravel path. Bees hum lazily between the flowers, and the air smells faintly of fresh soil. In one corner, a small wooden bench sits in the shade of a mango tree, perfect for reading during break time.

---

## 6. LOOK AND NOTICE

1. Which sentence tells you, in one line, what the whole paragraph is about?
2. Which words help you picture the garden (colours, sounds, smells)?
3. Do all the sentences stay on the same topic, or does one wander off?
4. Could you draw the garden just from reading this paragraph? What does that tell you about the description?

---

## 7. DISCOVER / EXPLICIT TEACHING

### Explicit explanation

A **paragraph** is a group of sentences that work together to develop **one main idea**. A well-built paragraph usually has:

| Part | Job |
|---|---|
| Topic sentence | States the main idea of the paragraph, usually first |
| Supporting details | Give more information, examples, or description about the main idea |
| Logical order | Arranges the sentences so ideas flow sensibly from one to the next |
| Unity | Every sentence relates to the topic sentence — nothing wanders off-topic |

**Descriptive writing** adds a further layer: instead of vague words, it uses **sensory details** — what something looks, sounds, smells, feels, and (sometimes) tastes like — to help the reader picture the scene clearly.

---

## 8. GRAMMAR WORD

## Grammar Word: TOPIC SENTENCE

A **topic sentence** is the sentence, usually the first in a paragraph, that clearly states the main idea the rest of the paragraph will develop.

### Examples

- "The school garden is a peaceful corner behind the main building." (introduces the topic: the garden, and its mood — peaceful)
- "Monday mornings at our house are always chaotic." (introduces the topic: Monday mornings, and the mood — chaotic)

### Non-examples / Contrast

- "The garden has flowers." (too plain and narrow — it does not set up a fuller main idea for the paragraph to develop)
- A paragraph with no topic sentence at all, where the reader must guess the main idea from a jumble of details.

### Quick Check

Which sentence would make a better topic sentence: "My dog is brown," or "My dog is the most playful member of our family"? *(The second — it sets up a main idea the rest of the paragraph can develop.)*

---

## 9. CORE EXPLANATION

**The topic sentence sets the direction**
→ Example: "Our classroom becomes a lively, colourful place every December." (topic: classroom in December)
→ Contrast: A paragraph that begins with an unrelated fact ("Yesterday I ate breakfast.") gives the reader no clue what the paragraph is really about.
→ Check: If I read only the first sentence, would I know what the whole paragraph is going to describe or explain?

**Supporting details develop the idea**
→ Example: After "Our classroom becomes a lively, colourful place every December," add details — paper snowflakes on the windows, a small tree in the corner, cheerful carols during break.
→ Contrast: A detail like "My pencil box is blue" does not support this topic sentence at all — it belongs in a different paragraph.
→ Check: Does each new sentence add something that helps explain or picture the topic sentence?

**Logical order guides the reader**
→ Example (spatial order): Describing a room from the door, to the walls, to the far corner.
→ Example (time order): Describing a morning routine from waking up to leaving for school.
→ Contrast: Jumping between "the bench in the shade" and then back to "the path near the gate" and then back to "the bench" again feels confusing and repetitive.
→ Check: Does one sentence lead naturally into the next — by space, by time, or by importance?

**Sensory detail creates vivid description**
→ Example: Instead of "The bakery smelled nice," write "The bakery smelled of warm, buttery bread fresh from the oven."
→ Contrast: Vague words like *nice, good, nice-looking* tell the reader almost nothing specific.
→ Check: Have I used a specific sight, sound, smell, touch, or taste detail instead of a vague word?

---

## 10. TYPES / CLASSIFICATIONS

### Ways to Order a Descriptive Paragraph

Name: Spatial order
Meaning: Describing details in the order they appear in space (e.g., top to bottom, near to far, left to right).
Example: "At the front of the classroom stands the teacher's desk. Behind it, a large green board covers the wall. Rows of wooden desks fill the rest of the room."
Contrast: Time order instead describes events as they happen one after another.
Quick Check: Which order would suit describing a tall tree from its roots to its top branches? *(spatial order)*

Name: Time order (chronological order)
Meaning: Describing details in the order they happened.
Example: "First, dark clouds gathered. Then, the wind picked up. Finally, the rain began to fall."
Contrast: Spatial order instead moves through a scene by location, not by time.
Quick Check: Which order would suit describing how a caterpillar becomes a butterfly? *(time order)*

### The Five Senses for Description

Name: Sensory details
Meaning: Specific details about how something looks, sounds, smells, feels, or tastes.
Example: sight — "golden sunlight"; sound — "the clatter of pots"; smell — "the sharp scent of chalk dust"; touch — "the rough bark of the tree"; taste — "the tangy sourness of a lemon"
Contrast: A description with only one sense used throughout (always sight) is flatter than one that mixes senses.
Quick Check: Which sense does "the crunch of dry leaves underfoot" appeal to? *(sound, and touch)*

---

## 11. WORKED EXAMPLE

**Task:** Write a short descriptive paragraph about a rainy day, using a topic sentence, at least three supporting details, and sensory language.

Step 1: Choose a clear topic sentence that sets the mood. *"The first rain of the season transformed our street in a matter of minutes."*

Step 2: Brainstorm sensory details — sight (dark clouds, puddles forming), sound (drumming on the roof, children laughing), smell (wet earth), touch (cool droplets on skin).

Step 3: Arrange the details in a logical order — here, time order works well, since the rain changes the street as it falls.

Step 4: Write the full paragraph, keeping every sentence connected to the topic.

Answer: "The first rain of the season transformed our street in a matter of minutes. Thick grey clouds rolled in, and within moments, fat drops began to drum steadily on our tin roof. The dry earth outside gave off a fresh, earthy smell as it soaked up the water. Children ran out barefoot, laughing and splashing through the puddles that had already begun to form along the road."

Why: The topic sentence sets up the idea (the rain transforming the street), each supporting detail adds a different sensory picture, and the details are arranged in the order they would actually happen.

---

## 12. COMPARE AND NOTICE

**Before (vague, no clear order):** "The market was busy. There were vegetables. I saw a boy. It was noisy. My mother bought onions. There was a dog."

**After (topic sentence, ordered, sensory):** "The morning market buzzed with activity. Vendors called out the prices of bright red tomatoes and leafy green spinach, stacked in neat piles. A small dog darted between the stalls, chasing a scrap of bread. Amid the noise, my mother calmly haggled over a bag of onions, her basket slowly filling with the week's vegetables."

What changed? A clear topic sentence was added, the details were arranged so the scene builds naturally, and vague words like "busy" and "noisy" were replaced with specific sights and sounds.
Why? The improved version lets the reader picture and almost hear the market, instead of just being told a list of facts about it.

---

## 13. GUIDED PRACTICE

**A. Identification**
Read the paragraph and underline the topic sentence.
1. "Our school library is my favourite room in the whole building. Tall wooden shelves are packed with colourful storybooks. Soft cushions are scattered near the window for quiet reading. A gentle hush always fills the air, broken only by the occasional turning of a page."

**B. Matching**
Match the vague sentence to its more descriptive version.

| Vague | Descriptive |
|---|---|
| "The food was nice." | ? |
| "The dog was big." | ? |
| "It was loud." | ? |

**C. Completion**
Add one sensory detail (sight, sound, or smell) to complete each sentence.
1. The kitchen was warm and ___________________.
2. The playground echoed with ___________________.

---

## 14. INDEPENDENT PRACTICE

1. Write a topic sentence for a paragraph about your classroom.
2. Add three supporting details (using at least one sensory detail) to the topic sentence you wrote.
3. Arrange your three details in a logical order (spatial or time order) and write the full paragraph.
4. **Reasoning:** Why might a paragraph without a topic sentence feel confusing to a reader, even if every individual sentence is grammatically correct?

---

## 15. GRAMMAR DETECTIVE

Student paragraph:

> "My birthday party was fun. We had a cake. My uncle fixed his bicycle yesterday. There were balloons everywhere. I got a new book as a present."

Questions:
1. What is wrong with this paragraph?
2. Which sentence should be removed, and why?
3. How could the paragraph be improved with more descriptive detail?

*(Guidance: The sentence about the uncle's bicycle does not belong — it breaks the unity of the paragraph. The remaining sentences could also be made more vivid: what colour were the balloons, what flavour was the cake, what was the new book about?)*

---

## 16. COMMON CONFUSION

### WATCH OUT!

Common confusion: Learners often write a list of unconnected facts (a "story in bullet points") rather than a flowing paragraph, or they include one or two sentences that do not relate to the topic sentence at all.

How to check: Ask, "Does every sentence in my paragraph relate back to my topic sentence? Does one sentence lead naturally into the next?"

Correct examples: A paragraph about a rainy day should not suddenly mention what someone had for breakfast three days earlier — that breaks unity, even if the sentence itself is grammatically correct.

---

## 17. LANGUAGE IN REAL LIFE

- **Diary entry:** "Today was the most exciting day of my summer holidays." (topic sentence, followed by descriptive detail)
- **Travel blog:** "The old fort stood silently against the orange evening sky, its stone walls worn smooth by centuries of wind." (sensory, descriptive)
- **School project:** "Our solar system project focused on the differences between the inner and outer planets." (clear topic sentence for an informative paragraph)
- **Story writing:** "The forest grew darker with every step, and the only sound was the crunch of dry leaves beneath our feet." (spatial and sensory description)

---

## 18. SPEAKING / LISTENING

### Pair Talk

Student A: Describe a place you know well (your room, a park, a shop) aloud for thirty seconds, using at least two sensory details.
Student B: Listen carefully, then repeat back the topic (the main idea) and one sensory detail you remember. Then switch roles.

---

## 19. WRITING CONNECTION

Write a descriptive paragraph (5-7 sentences) about a place that is special to you. Begin with a clear topic sentence, arrange your supporting details in a logical order (spatial or time order), and include at least three different sensory details.

---

## 21. EXACT EDUCATIONAL GRAPHIC

`TYPESET GRAPHIC — NOT GENERATIVE IMAGE`

- Dimensions: full width, 5-row reference chart
- Exact labels: "Sense", "Question It Answers", "Example Detail"
- Data: Sight ("What do I see?", "golden afternoon light"); Sound ("What do I hear?", "the clatter of pots"); Smell ("What do I smell?", "fresh-cut grass"); Touch ("What does it feel like?", "the rough bark of the tree"); Taste ("What does it taste like?", "the tangy sourness of a lemon")
- Layout: reference table placed in Section 10, one row per sense

---

## 22. QUICK CHECK

1. What is a topic sentence?
2. Name two ways a paragraph's details can be logically ordered.
3. Rewrite this vague sentence with one sensory detail: "The soup was nice."
4. True or False: Every sentence in a good paragraph should relate back to the topic sentence.
5. Which sense does the phrase "the sharp scent of chalk dust" appeal to?

---

## 23. THINK DEEPER

1. Why do you think a paragraph with no clear topic sentence can still contain several correct sentences, yet still feel confusing to read?
2. Can a paragraph use more than one type of order at once (for example, mostly spatial order but with one detail placed for extra effect)? Discuss with a partner.
3. Why might a writer deliberately choose only one or two senses (instead of all five) to describe a very quiet, still scene?

---

## 24. CREATE YOUR OWN

- Write your own topic sentence about a season, a festival, or a hobby, then build a full paragraph around it using logical order and sensory detail.
- Create a "Grammar Detective" paragraph of your own with one unrelated sentence hidden inside it, for a partner to find and remove.

---

## 25. SUMMARY

- A **paragraph** is a group of sentences that develop one main idea.
- The **topic sentence** usually comes first and states the main idea clearly.
- **Supporting details** add information, examples, or description that relate directly to the topic sentence.
- Details should be arranged in a **logical order** — often spatial order or time order.
- **Sensory details** (sight, sound, smell, touch, taste) make descriptive writing vivid and specific.
- A well-built paragraph has **unity** — every sentence belongs to the same main idea.

---

## 26. KEY TERMS

| Term | Simple Meaning |
|---|---|
| Paragraph | A group of sentences developing one main idea |
| Topic sentence | The sentence that states the paragraph's main idea |
| Supporting detail | A sentence that develops or explains the topic sentence |
| Spatial order | Arranging details by location in space |
| Time order (chronological order) | Arranging details in the order they happened |
| Sensory detail | A specific detail about sight, sound, smell, touch, or taste |
| Unity | Every sentence in a paragraph relating to the same main idea |

---

## 27. SELF-CHECK

I can:

- [ ] identify the topic sentence in a paragraph.
- [ ] write a clear topic sentence for a given subject.
- [ ] add relevant supporting details to a topic sentence.
- [ ] arrange paragraph details in a logical order.
- [ ] use sensory details to write vivid description.
- [ ] edit a paragraph to remove an unrelated sentence.

---

## 28. PRACTICE ZONE

A. **Identify** [R] — Underline the topic sentence in five given paragraphs.
B. **Classify** [U] — Sort a list of descriptive phrases by which sense they appeal to.
C. **Complete** [U] — Add supporting details to given topic sentences.
D. **Correct** [A] — Remove the unrelated sentence from a paragraph and fix its order.
E. **Rearrange/Transform** [A] — Reorder a scrambled paragraph into logical spatial or time order.
F. **Explain** [AN] — Explain why a given sentence does or does not belong in a paragraph.
G. **Apply** [A] — Write an original descriptive paragraph about a chosen place.
H. **Create** [C] — Write a full paragraph with a topic sentence, ordered details, and sensory language.

---

## 29. REAL-LIFE TASK

Choose a room in your home. Write a five-sentence descriptive paragraph about it, starting with a topic sentence and including at least two sensory details. Read it aloud to a family member and ask if they can picture the room clearly.

---

## 30. TEACHER SUPPORT NOTE

Likely misconception: Students often write a list of separate facts rather than a connected paragraph, and may include off-topic sentences because they wrote them as they thought of them, without checking unity afterwards.

Support strategy: Teach a simple "one paragraph, one idea" check — after drafting, students underline the topic sentence and then check whether every other sentence connects back to it, crossing out or moving anything that does not.

Extension: Introduce order of importance (least to most important detail, or most to least) as a third possible ordering strategy for more advanced learners.

Challenge: Ask advanced learners to write the same descriptive paragraph twice — once in spatial order and once in time order — and discuss how the effect changes.

---

## 31. ANSWER KEY DATA

**Section 22 (Quick Check):**
1. The sentence, usually first in a paragraph, that clearly states the main idea the rest of the paragraph will develop.
2. Spatial order and time (chronological) order.
3. Any reasonable sensory rewrite (e.g., "The soup was warm and spicy, with a rich, smoky aroma.")
4. True
5. Smell

**Section 13 (Guided Practice A):** "Our school library is my favourite room in the whole building."

**Section 13 (Guided Practice B):** Any reasonable, specific descriptive rewrite for each vague sentence (e.g., "The food was nice" → "The food was warm, spicy, and full of flavour.")

For open-ended items (Sections 14, 19, 24, 29): accept any grammatically correct paragraph with a clear topic sentence, relevant supporting details in a logical order, and at least the requested number of sensory details. Evaluation criteria: presence and clarity of topic sentence, relevance/unity of supporting details, logical ordering, use of specific sensory language, original content.

---
