# CHAPTER 28: LANGUAGE WORKSHOP

## 0. CHAPTER METADATA

**Chapter Number:** 28
**Chapter Title:** Language Workshop: One Passage, Every Skill
**Unit:** 9 — Integrated Language Workshop
**Primary Concept:** Using a single short reading passage as the shared source for integrated practice across vocabulary, grammar identification, sentence transformation, editing, and writing — rather than teaching any single new rule.
**Secondary Concepts:** Reading for language features (not just meaning), pulling vocabulary from context, transforming sentences drawn from a real passage, editing within a reading-based task, writing that responds to and extends a passage.
**Prerequisites:** This chapter deliberately draws on the whole book, and is built directly on Chapter 27's five-step self-editing checklist (agreement, tense, punctuation, word choice, sentence construction), as well as Chapter 17 (synonyms/antonyms), Chapter 20 (sentence combination/transformation), and Chapter 24 (paragraph writing).
**Estimated Time:** 4-5 hours
**Estimated Pages:** 12-15
**Difficulty:** High (integrated review — no single skill is new, but the workshop format asks learners to move between skills within one passage)
**Writing Link:** Writing that responds directly to a passage, reusing its topic, vocabulary, or structure.
**Speaking/Listening Link:** Discussing a passage aloud before working with it in writing; reading a corrected passage aloud to check it sounds right.
**Vocabulary Load:** Medium (drawn entirely from the workshop passage itself, not new taught vocabulary)

---

## 1. PEDAGOGICAL PURPOSE

**Why is this concept being taught here?**
Chapter 27 taught learners to hunt for errors across five categories at once. This chapter takes integration one step further: instead of starting from an error-filled passage, it starts from a **single short reading passage** — the kind of connected text learners meet in real books, notices, and articles — and uses that one passage as the shared source for every kind of task in this book: finding vocabulary in context, identifying grammar at work, transforming sentences, editing, and writing a response. This mirrors how a "language workshop" period works in a real classroom: one shared text, many different things to notice and do with it.

**What should the learner be able to do with it?**
The learner can:
- Read a short passage and locate specific vocabulary, grammar features, and sentence types within it.
- Explain the meaning of an unfamiliar word using context clues from the passage.
- Transform sentences taken directly from the passage (e.g., active to passive, direct to reported, statement to question) without changing their meaning.
- Edit a related passage using the checklist from Chapter 27.
- Write a short piece that responds to, continues, or is modelled on the workshop passage.

---

## 2. LEARNING OUTCOMES

The learner can:

- [ ] read a short passage and answer simple comprehension questions about it.
- [ ] find a synonym, antonym, or word meaning using context clues from the passage.
- [ ] identify a named grammar feature (a tense, a type of sentence, a part of speech) within the passage.
- [ ] transform a sentence taken from the passage without changing its meaning.
- [ ] edit a short passage using the five-step checklist from Chapter 27.
- [ ] write a short piece that responds to or is modelled on the workshop passage.

---

## 3. PREREQUISITE CHECK

### Quick Check

Look back at Chapter 27's five-step checklist. Without looking, try to name as many of the five steps as you can.

### Remember This

You already have every tool this chapter needs: vocabulary skills from Chapters 17-19, grammar knowledge from every earlier chapter, the editing checklist from Chapter 27, and paragraph-writing skills from Chapter 24. This chapter simply brings them all to the same short passage, one task at a time — like a workshop where every tool sits on the same table.

---

## 4. CHAPTER OPENER

Ms. Sharma has photocopied a short passage for the whole class and pinned extra questions around the room — some about meaning, some about grammar, some about writing.

**Ms. Sharma:** "Today we are not learning anything brand new. Instead, we are going to treat this one short passage like a workshop table. We'll pick up a different tool each time — first a vocabulary tool, then a grammar tool, then an editing tool, then a writing tool — but we'll keep coming back to the very same passage."

**Rohan:** "So it's like using the same photo for lots of different questions?"

**Maya:** "Yes — and I bet the passage will start to feel really familiar by the end!"

**Ms. Sharma:** "Exactly, Maya. Let's begin."

---

## 5. LANGUAGE EXPERIENCE

**The Workshop Passage: "The Kite Festival"**

> Every January, the rooftops of Rohan's town fill with colour. Families climb up before sunrise, carrying kites of every shape and size. Rohan's grandfather, who has flown kites since he was a boy, taught him how to release the string at just the right moment. "Feel the wind first," his grandfather always says, "before you let go." By afternoon, the sky is crowded with paper diamonds, dipping and soaring above the narrow streets. Rohan's kite, a bright red one shaped like a fish, climbed higher than any other kite on the roof. He grinned with pride, and for a moment, he forgot to hold on tightly — the string slipped, and his kite drifted silently away over the rooftops.

---

## 6. LOOK AND NOTICE

1. Who are the characters in this passage, and where does it take place?
2. Find one word in the passage you think another word could replace without changing the meaning much.
3. Find one sentence written as a piece of direct speech. How do you know it is direct speech?
4. Find one verb in the past tense and one verb in the present tense. Why do you think the passage uses both?

---

## 7. DISCOVER / EXPLICIT TEACHING

### Explicit explanation

A language workshop moves through the same passage several times, each time with a different purpose:

| Pass | Purpose | Skill Area |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read for meaning | Comprehension |
| 2 | Notice vocabulary and context clues | Vocabulary (Ch. 17-19) |
| 3 | Identify grammar features at work | Grammar (whole book) |
| 4 | Transform sentences from the passage | Sentence craft (Ch. 20-22) |
| 5 | Edit a related passage | Editing (Ch. 27) |
| 6 | Write a response | Writing (Ch. 24, 26) |

Rereading the same text for different purposes is a genuine skill — a single passage almost always has more to notice than a single reading reveals.

---

## 8. GRAMMAR WORD

## Grammar Word: CONTEXT CLUE

A **context clue** is a hint in the surrounding sentence or passage that helps a reader work out the meaning of an unfamiliar word, without needing a dictionary.

### Examples

- "The sky was **crowded** with paper diamonds, dipping and soaring above the streets." — the surrounding words (many kites, a busy sky) suggest "crowded" means "full of many things."
- "Rohan's kite **drifted** silently away." — "silently" and "away" suggest slow, gentle movement, helping explain "drifted."

### Non-examples / Contrast

- Guessing a word's meaning purely from how it *sounds*, without checking the surrounding sentence, often leads to the wrong meaning.

### Quick Check

In the workshop passage, what context clues help you guess the meaning of "soaring"? *(Words like "sky," "dipping," and "above the streets" suggest flying high and smoothly.)*

---

## 9. CORE EXPLANATION

**Reading for vocabulary in context**
→ Example: "Families climb up before sunrise" — the surrounding words (rooftops, kites, January) help confirm "climb" means going upward, here onto a roof.
→ Contrast: Looking up every unfamiliar word in a dictionary before finishing a sentence interrupts reading; context clues let a reader keep moving and often get close enough to the meaning.
→ Check: Can I guess a sensible meaning for an unfamiliar word just from the words around it?

**Reading for grammar features**
→ Example: "Rohan's kite... **climbed** higher than any other kite" is past tense, matching the story's narrative frame; "his grandfather always **says**" is present tense, showing a habitual, still-true action.
→ Contrast: Not every sentence in a passage will use the same tense — narrative sentences and quoted habitual sayings can differ for a good reason (Chapter 15).
→ Check: Can I name the tense, sentence type, or part of speech being asked about, and point to the exact word in the passage that shows it?

**Transforming sentences from a real passage**
→ Example: Active — "His grandfather taught him how to release the string." → Passive — "He was taught by his grandfather how to release the string."
→ Contrast: A transformation should keep the original meaning; changing "release" to "hold," for instance, would no longer be a fair transformation of the same idea.
→ Check: Does my transformed sentence still mean exactly what the original passage sentence meant?

**Editing within a workshop task**
→ Example: A related passage (not the model passage itself) is deliberately seeded with agreement, tense, punctuation, word-choice, and sentence-construction errors for practice, exactly as in Chapter 27.
→ Contrast: Editing the model passage itself would be pointless, since it contains no errors — workshop editing tasks always use a *related but separate* passage.
→ Check: Am I applying the full five-step checklist, not stopping after the first error I notice?

**Writing a response to the passage**
→ Example: Continuing "The Kite Festival" by imagining where Rohan's lost kite eventually lands, using consistent past tense and at least one piece of correctly punctuated dialogue.
→ Contrast: A response task is not a summary — it should add something new (a continuation, a different character's viewpoint, a diary entry about the same day) rather than simply repeating the passage's own sentences.
→ Check: Does my writing genuinely respond to or extend the passage, using the grammar and vocabulary skills from this book?

---

## 10. TYPES / CLASSIFICATIONS

### Comprehension Questions

Name: Comprehension questions
Meaning: Questions that check whether the reader understood the passage's events, characters, and ideas.
Example: "Why did Rohan's kite drift away?" "What does his grandfather always tell him?"
Contrast: Comprehension questions are about *what happened*, unlike grammar questions, which are about *how* the passage is written.
Quick Check: Is "What colour was Rohan's kite?" a comprehension question or a grammar question? *(comprehension)*

### Language-Focus Questions

Name: Language-focus questions
Meaning: Questions that direct attention to a specific grammar or vocabulary feature within the passage, rather than to its meaning alone.
Example: "Find one adjective describing the kite." "Find one sentence in the passive voice, or explain why there isn't one."
Contrast: A language-focus question always has a specific, checkable answer pointing to particular words in the text, unlike an open comprehension question that can be answered in the reader's own words.
Quick Check: Is "Find a verb in the past tense" a comprehension question or a language-focus question? *(language-focus)*

---

## 11. WORKED EXAMPLE

**Task:** Using the workshop passage, (1) explain the meaning of "grinned" using context, and (2) transform the sentence "His grandfather taught him how to release the string" into the passive voice.

Step 1 (Vocabulary): Look at the surrounding words — "He grinned with pride." The context (pride, a moment of success) suggests "grinned" means smiled widely, happily.

Step 2 (Grammar transformation): Identify the doer and receiver. Doer: "his grandfather." Receiver: "him," and the thing taught: "how to release the string."

Step 3: Make the receiver the new subject and add the correct form of "be" plus the past participle.

Answer:
1. "Grinned" means smiled widely and happily, shown by the context clue "with pride."
2. "He was taught by his grandfather how to release the string."

Why: Both answers were found by rereading the same short passage with two different purposes — first for word meaning, then for a specific grammar transformation — exactly as a language workshop asks learners to do.

---

## 12. COMPARE AND NOTICE

**Reading once, for meaning only:** A reader finishes "The Kite Festival" and can say what happened: a boy's kite flew away.

**Reading again, for language:** The same reader now notices that "always says" is present tense (a habit), that "climbed," "grinned," and "drifted" are past tense (the story's events), that "release" could be replaced by "let go" (a synonym already used in the grandfather's own words), and that the sentence about the grandfather's teaching could become passive.

What changed? Nothing in the passage changed — but the *purpose* of reading changed, revealing details a single reading for meaning would miss.
Why? Each rereading, with a specific purpose, uncovers a different layer of the same text — this is the core workshop skill.

---

## 13. GUIDED PRACTICE

**A. Identification**
Using the workshop passage, find one example of each.
1. A word that could be replaced by a synonym without changing the meaning much.
2. A verb in the simple present tense.
3. A piece of direct speech.

**B. Matching**
Match the workshop pass to its purpose.

| Pass | Purpose |
|---|---|
| Vocabulary pass | ? |
| Grammar pass | ? |
| Writing pass | ? |

**C. Completion**
Transform this sentence from the passage into the passive voice: "Rohan's grandfather taught him how to release the string."

---

## 14. INDEPENDENT PRACTICE

1. Find two more words in the workshop passage and explain their meaning using a context clue from the surrounding sentence.
2. Imagine Rohan's grandfather also said, "I am proud of you." Change this into reported speech, as you learned in Chapter 22.
3. Identify one adjective and one adverb from the passage, and explain what each one describes.
4. **Reasoning:** Why might rereading the same passage several times, each time with a different purpose, help you notice more than reading it only once very carefully?

---

## 15. GRAMMAR DETECTIVE

A related passage, written by Leo the parrot, describing a different kite festival:

> "Last year my family goes to the kite festival too. We sees hundreds of kites in the sky! My kites string breaked and it flied away over the rooftops. Everyone were laughing and I is laughing too."

Questions:
1. Find at least four errors, and sort each one into a checklist category from Chapter 27 (agreement, tense, punctuation, word choice, sentence construction).
2. Correct the whole passage.
3. Explain which earlier chapter's rule fixes each error.

*(Guidance: "goes" → tense/agreement ("went," Ch. 14); "We sees" → agreement ("see," Ch. 13, though tense should also be past: "saw"); "My kites string" → word choice/possession ("My kite's string," Ch. 16); "breaked" → tense, irregular verb ("broke," Ch. 14); "flied" → tense, irregular verb ("flew," Ch. 14); "Everyone were" → agreement ("Everyone was," Ch. 13); "I is" → agreement ("I was," Ch. 13, matching the past-tense story). Corrected passage: "Last year my family went to the kite festival too. We saw hundreds of kites in the sky! My kite's string broke and it flew away over the rooftops. Everyone was laughing and I was laughing too.")*

---

## 16. COMMON CONFUSION

### WATCH OUT!

Common confusion: Learners sometimes treat every workshop question as a comprehension question, answering "what happened" even when the task actually asks about a specific grammar or vocabulary feature.

How to check: Ask, "Is this question asking me what the passage *means*, or is it asking me to point to a specific *word or sentence type* in the passage?"

Correct examples: "Why did the kite fly away?" needs an answer about events (comprehension). "Find a verb in the past tense" needs a specific word pointed to in the text (language-focus) — not a retelling of the story.

---

## 17. LANGUAGE IN REAL LIFE

- **Classroom reading circle:** Students read the same short story together, then take turns answering comprehension, vocabulary, and grammar questions about it.
- **Newspaper study:** A teacher uses one short news article to teach new vocabulary, spot verb tenses, and prompt a short written response, all from the same clipping.
- **Language exam paper:** Many English exams use exactly this workshop structure — one passage, followed by a mixture of comprehension, vocabulary, and grammar questions.
- **Book club discussion:** Readers reread a favourite chapter, this time noticing the author's word choices rather than just the plot.

---

## 18. SPEAKING / LISTENING

### Pair Talk

Student A: Read the workshop passage aloud to a partner.
Student B: Listen and then ask Student A one comprehension question and one language-focus question (e.g., "Find a word in the past tense") based on what you just heard. Then switch roles.

---

## 19. WRITING CONNECTION

Choose **one** of the following, responding to "The Kite Festival":
- Continue the story: write 4-6 sentences describing what happens to Rohan's kite after it drifts away, keeping the tense consistent with the original passage.
- Write a short diary entry (4-6 sentences) from Rohan's point of view about the day his kite flew away, beginning with the date.
- Rewrite the passage's final two sentences from the grandfather's point of view instead of Rohan's, changing pronouns as needed.

## 21. EXACT EDUCATIONAL GRAPHIC

## 22. QUICK CHECK

1. What is a context clue?
2. Name the six workshop passes, in order.
3. Is "Why did Rohan's kite fly away?" a comprehension question or a language-focus question?
4. Change this sentence from the workshop passage into the passive voice: "His grandfather taught him how to release the string."
5. Why should an editing task in a language workshop use a passage separate from the original model passage?

---

## 23. THINK DEEPER

1. Why might reading the same short passage five or six times, each with a different purpose, teach you more than reading five completely different passages once each?
2. Can a single sentence answer both a comprehension question and a language-focus question at the same time? Try to find one in the workshop passage.
3. Why do you think many English exams are built around exactly this "one passage, many questions" structure?

---

## 24. CREATE YOUR OWN

- Choose any short passage from an earlier chapter in this book (or write a new short passage of your own, 4-6 sentences) and write one comprehension question, one vocabulary question, and one grammar-transformation question about it.
- Write a short passage (4-5 sentences) containing two or three deliberate errors from different checklist categories, for a partner to find and correct using the Chapter 27 checklist.

---

## 25. SUMMARY

- A **language workshop** returns to the same short passage several times, each time with a different purpose.
- **Context clues** are hints in the surrounding text that help explain an unfamiliar word's meaning.
- **Comprehension questions** ask about meaning and events; **language-focus questions** ask about specific grammar or vocabulary features.
- Sentences from a real passage can be **transformed** (active/passive, direct/reported, statement/question) without changing their meaning.
- Editing tasks use a **related but separate** passage, checked with the five-step checklist from Chapter 27.
- Writing tasks should genuinely **respond to or extend** the passage, not just repeat it.

---

## 26. KEY TERMS

| Term | Simple Meaning |
|---|---|
| Language workshop | A structured way of using one passage for several different language tasks |
| Context clue | A hint in the surrounding text that helps explain a word's meaning |
| Comprehension question | A question about what happened or what a passage means |
| Language-focus question | A question about a specific grammar or vocabulary feature in a passage |
| Transformation | Rewriting a sentence in a different grammatical form without changing its meaning |

---

## 27. SELF-CHECK

I can:

- [ ] read a short passage and answer comprehension questions about it.
- [ ] use context clues to explain an unfamiliar word's meaning.
- [ ] identify a named grammar feature within a passage.
- [ ] transform a sentence from a passage without changing its meaning.
- [ ] edit a related passage using the five-step checklist.
- [ ] write a short piece that responds to or extends a passage.

---

## 28. PRACTICE ZONE

A. **Identify** [R] — Find examples of five different word classes or tenses within the workshop passage.
B. **Classify** [U] — Sort a list of questions about the passage into "comprehension" and "language-focus."
C. **Complete** [U] — Answer context-clue vocabulary questions about the passage.
D. **Correct** [A] — Edit a related, error-filled passage using the five-step checklist.
E. **Rearrange/Transform** [A] — Transform three sentences taken from the passage (active/passive, direct/reported, statement/question).
F. **Explain** [AN] — Explain how a context clue in the passage helped you work out a word's meaning.
G. **Apply** [A] — Write a short response to the passage (continuation, diary entry, or viewpoint change).
H. **Create** [C] — Build your own mini language-workshop set (passage plus three questions) for a partner.

---

## 29. REAL-LIFE TASK

Choose a short passage from a storybook, magazine, or newspaper at home (with permission). Read it once for meaning, then reread it to find one new word (and guess its meaning from context), one verb tense, and one sentence you could transform into a different form.

---

## 30. TEACHER SUPPORT NOTE

Likely misconception: Students often answer every question about a passage as if it were a comprehension question, even when the task specifically asks for a grammar feature, a transformation, or an edit — because comprehension is the most familiar type of reading question from earlier schooling.

Support strategy: Before setting any workshop task, have students identify aloud what *kind* of question is being asked (comprehension vs. language-focus) before attempting an answer, to build the habit of reading the task itself carefully.

Extension: Introduce longer workshop passages (6-10 sentences) later in the year, once learners are comfortable moving between the six passes on a short passage.

Challenge: Ask advanced learners to write their own six-pass workshop set (comprehension, vocabulary, grammar, transformation, editing companion passage, and writing prompt) around a passage of their own choosing.

---

## 31. ANSWER KEY DATA

**Section 22 (Quick Check):**
1. A hint in the surrounding sentence or passage that helps a reader work out an unfamiliar word's meaning.
2. Read for meaning; notice vocabulary/context clues; identify grammar features; transform sentences; edit a related passage; write a response.
3. Comprehension question.
4. "He was taught by his grandfather how to release the string."
5. Because the original model passage contains no errors to find — an editing task needs a separate passage that has been deliberately seeded with mistakes to correct.

**Section 13 (Guided Practice B):** Vocabulary pass → notice context clues and word meaning; Grammar pass → identify grammar features at work; Writing pass → respond to or extend the passage

**Section 13 (Guided Practice C):** "His grandfather's string was released with his help" is not correct — the accurate transformation is: "He was taught by his grandfather how to release the string."

For open-ended items (Sections 14, 19, 24, 29): accept any response that correctly completes the requested task (context-clue explanation, transformation, response writing) using the workshop passage or the learner's own chosen passage. Evaluation criteria: correct use of context clues, correct and meaning-preserving transformation, genuine engagement with (not mere repetition of) the source passage, original content where writing is requested.

# CHAPTER 29: REVISION AND PRACTICE TESTS

## 0. CHAPTER METADATA

**Chapter Number:** 29
**Chapter Title:** Revision and Practice Tests: Showing What You Know
**Unit:** 9 — Integrated Language Workshop
**Primary Concept:** Consolidating the whole book through balanced, mixed-format practice tests covering grammar, vocabulary, sentence construction, editing, and writing together, mirroring how a real end-of-year assessment is structured.
**Secondary Concepts:** Test-taking strategy (reading instructions carefully, managing time across sections, checking work before finishing), self-marking against a full answer key, identifying personal areas for further revision.
**Prerequisites:** This chapter deliberately draws on the entire book. It is the final chapter and assumes every earlier chapter, especially Chapters 13-28, has already been studied.
**Estimated Time:** 5-6 hours (two full practice tests plus review)
**Estimated Pages:** 16-20
**Difficulty:** High (comprehensive, mixed-skill review — no single skill is new)
**Writing Link:** Producing complete, independent writing under test-like conditions, without the scaffolding of a Language Experience section.
**Speaking/Listening Link:** Discussing test answers with a partner afterwards, explaining reasoning rather than only stating the answer.
**Vocabulary Load:** Medium (drawn from across the whole book, with no new vocabulary taught)

---

## 1. PEDAGOGICAL PURPOSE

**Why is this concept being taught here?**
This is the final chapter of the book, and its purpose is different from every chapter before it, including Chapter 27 (Grammar Detective) and Chapter 28 (Language Workshop). Those chapters still taught learners *how* to integrate skills, with worked examples and scaffolded practice. This chapter instead offers learners a chance to **show what they know**, independently, in a format close to a real class test — while still including a full answer key and guidance for what to revise next. Per `ChapterTemplate.md` Section 38 ("Do Not Force the Template"), this chapter deliberately omits some scaffolding sections (Language Experience, Look and Notice, Grammar Word, Worked Example) that do not suit a revision-and-testing chapter, and instead expands the practice materials themselves.

**What should the learner be able to do with it?**
The learner can:
- Complete a full, mixed-section practice test independently, covering grammar, vocabulary, sentence construction, editing, and writing.
- Use test-taking strategies: reading instructions carefully, allocating time sensibly, and checking answers before finishing.
- Mark their own or a partner's completed test against a full answer key.
- Identify, from their own results, one or two areas that would benefit from further revision, and know which earlier chapter to revisit.

---

## 2. LEARNING OUTCOMES

The learner can:

- [ ] complete a full mixed-section practice test within a set time.
- [ ] apply grammar, vocabulary, sentence-construction, editing, and writing skills from across the whole book without additional teaching support.
- [ ] mark completed answers accurately against a provided answer key.
- [ ] identify personal strengths and one or two areas for further revision.
- [ ] locate the specific earlier chapter that covers any topic they need to revise.

---

## 3. PREREQUISITE CHECK

### Quick Check

Before beginning, look back at the Key Terms tables in Chapters 13-28. Choose any three terms and, without checking the definitions, try to explain each one in your own words.

### Remember This

This chapter does not teach anything new. Everything in the two practice tests that follow has already been taught somewhere earlier in this book. If a question feels unfamiliar, that is a signal to revisit the relevant chapter afterwards — not a sign that something new is expected of you here.

---

## 4. CHAPTER OPENER

It is the last week of term, and Ms. Sharma hands out two papers, face down, to the whole class.

**Ms. Sharma:** "Today and next week, we are going to do something a little different. These papers cover everything we have learned this year — nouns all the way through to paragraph writing. I want you to treat this like a real test: read every instruction carefully, manage your time, and check your work before you finish."

**Rohan:** *(a little nervous)* "What if I get something wrong?"

**Ms. Sharma:** "Then you'll know exactly what to revise before the real exam — which is the whole point! A practice test is not there to catch you out. It's there to show you, clearly, what you already know well and what still needs a little more practice."

**Maya:** "That actually makes it feel less scary."

---

## 5. TEST-TAKING STRATEGY

Before beginning either practice test, keep these strategies in mind:

| Strategy | Why It Helps |
|---|---|
| Read every instruction fully before answering | Some sections ask for more than one thing (e.g., "correct the sentence *and* explain why") |
| Attempt every section, even briefly | A partial answer usually earns more than a blank one |
| Watch the time allotted for each section | Spending too long on one section can leave too little time for others |
| Leave a few minutes at the end to check your work | Many small errors (a missing full stop, a wrong verb ending) are easy to catch on a second look |
| If a question feels unfamiliar, move on and return to it later | Getting stuck on one question can cost time you need elsewhere |

---

## 6. PRACTICE TEST A

**Suggested time: 60 minutes. Total marks: 50.**

### Section 1 — Grammar Identification (10 marks)

For each sentence, identify the part of speech, tense, or grammar feature asked for.

1. Underline the noun and circle the verb: *The children played happily in the garden.* (2 marks)
2. What tense is this sentence? *"Maya will visit her cousin next week."* (1 mark)
3. Identify the adjective and the noun it describes: *The clever fox tricked the crow.* (2 marks)
4. Is the underlined verb an action verb or a verb of being? *Leo **is** a clever parrot.* (1 mark)
5. Identify the conjunction and explain what relationship it shows: *"I wanted to play outside, but it started raining."* (2 marks)
6. Choose the correct article: *I saw ___ (a/an) elephant at the zoo.* (1 mark)
7. Is this sentence active or passive? *"The cake was baked by Maya."* (1 mark)

### Section 2 — Vocabulary (10 marks)

1. Give one synonym and one antonym for "happy." (2 marks)
2. Fill in the correct homophone: *___ (Their/There/They're) going to the market.* (1 mark)
3. Fill in the correct homophone: *___ (Its/It's) raining today.* (1 mark)
4. Add the prefix "un-" to "kind" and explain the new word's meaning. (2 marks)
5. Add the suffix "-ful" to "care" and use the new word in a sentence of your own. (2 marks)
6. Rewrite this sentence, replacing the vague word with a more precise one: *"The food was nice."* (2 marks)

### Section 3 — Subject-Verb Agreement and Tense (10 marks)

1. Choose the correct verb: *The basket of apples (is/are) heavy.* (1 mark)
2. Correct this sentence: *"The children plays outside every afternoon."* (2 marks)
3. Correct this sentence: *"Yesterday, we go to the fair and we eated ice cream."* (3 marks)
4. Complete with the correct present continuous form: *Look! The dog ___ (bark) at the gate.* (1 mark)
5. Rewrite this sentence in the future tense: *"Leo eats seeds."* (1 mark)
6. Correct the tense-consistency error: *"Every day, Maya feeds her fish. Yesterday, she cleans the tank."* (2 marks)

### Section 4 — Editing (10 marks)

Read the passage below and correct all the errors you can find (agreement, tense, punctuation, word choice, and sentence construction). There are eight errors in total.

> "yesterday my family go to the beach. we sees lots of shells and my brother collect some. its a very hot day and everyone were tired by the evening. i loves the beach."

### Section 5 — Writing (10 marks)

Write a short paragraph (5-7 sentences) describing a place that is special to you. Include:
- a clear topic sentence,
- at least two sensory details,
- consistent tense throughout.

---

## 7. PRACTICE TEST B

**Suggested time: 60 minutes. Total marks: 50.**

### Section 1 — Grammar Identification (10 marks)

1. Underline the subject and circle the predicate: *The old clock struck thirteen.* (2 marks)
2. Identify the preposition and its kind (place, time, or direction): *"The meeting will start at 10 o'clock."* (2 marks)
3. Identify the pronoun and say whether it is a subject or object pronoun: *"Rohan is waiting for us."* (2 marks)
4. What kind of sentence is this — statement, question, command, or exclamation? *"What a wonderful surprise!"* (1 mark)
5. Identify the adverb and the word it describes: *"She sang beautifully."* (2 marks)
6. Change this active sentence into the passive voice: *"The teacher checked our homework."* (1 mark)

### Section 2 — Vocabulary (10 marks)

1. Sort these words into three pairs, and say whether each pair is a synonym pair or an antonym pair: *happy, sad, joyful, tiny, huge, cheerful.* (3 marks)
2. Fill in the correct homophone: *I have ___ (to/too/two) pencils.* (1 mark)
3. What one word means "a person who cannot hear"? (1 mark)
4. Explain the difference in meaning between "careful" and "careless." (2 marks)
5. Rewrite this sentence to remove the repeated word: *"The tall boy climbed the tall tree to see the tall building."* (3 marks)

### Section 3 — Sentence Construction and Transformation (10 marks)

1. Combine these two sentences using a suitable joining word: *"It was raining. We stayed inside."* (2 marks)
2. Change this sentence from direct to reported speech: *Maya said, "I am tired."* (2 marks)
3. Change this sentence from reported to direct speech, adding correct punctuation: *Rohan said that he was hungry.* (2 marks)
4. Expand this simple sentence with two details (when and where): *"The children laughed."* (2 marks)
5. Fix the word order: *"Quickly the girl the ball threw."* (2 marks)

### Section 4 — Editing (10 marks)

Read the passage below and correct all the errors you can find (agreement, tense, punctuation, word choice, and sentence construction). There are nine errors in total.

> "my sister and me is going to the library. we reads books there every saturday. last week she borrow three books and I borrows two. its always quiet inside and everyone whisper. I loves the library."

### Section 5 — Writing (10 marks)

Choose **one**:
- Write a short informal letter (6-8 lines) to a friend, telling them about something exciting that happened recently. Include all five parts of an informal letter.
- Write a short story completion (5-7 sentences) continuing this opener: *"The old clock in the hallway struck thirteen, and that was when everything changed."* Keep your tense consistent throughout, and include at least one line of correctly punctuated dialogue.

---

## 8. SELF-MARKING GUIDANCE

After completing a practice test, mark your own work (or swap with a partner) using the Answer Key Data in Section 12. As you mark:

- Tick each answer that matches the key, or is a reasonable equivalent for open-ended items.
- For any answer that does not match, note which **chapter** covers that rule (the Answer Key Data names the chapter for every grammar and editing item).
- Add up your marks for each section separately, not just an overall total — this shows you *which kind* of task needs more practice, not only how many marks you scored.

## 10. GRAMMAR DETECTIVE

**A Test-Marking Mix-Up**

Rohan marked his own Practice Test A editing passage (Section 6, Section 4) and only found five of the eight errors, missing the tense mistakes because he had already found two punctuation mistakes and stopped checking closely.

Questions:
1. Which checklist habit from Chapter 27 would have helped Rohan catch all eight errors?
2. Why might finding a few errors early in a passage make it easy to stop looking for more?
3. What could Rohan do differently next time he self-marks a piece of editing?

*(Guidance: Rohan should have used the full five-step checklist from Chapter 27 — checking the passage once per category (agreement, tense, punctuation, word choice, sentence construction) — rather than stopping once he felt he had "found enough" errors. Finding some errors early can create a false sense that the check is complete; a systematic, category-by-category recheck avoids this.)*

---

## 11. THINK DEEPER

1. Why might completing a practice test under timed conditions feel different from doing the same questions with no time limit? Which do you think helps you learn more, and why?
2. If you scored well in the Grammar Identification section but poorly in Writing, what might that tell you about how you should spend your revision time?
3. Why do you think real class tests almost always mix several different kinds of task (identification, correction, writing) instead of testing only one skill at a time?

## 12. ANSWER KEY DATA

### Practice Test A

**Section 1 — Grammar Identification**
1. Noun: *children* (or *garden*); Verb: *played* (Ch. 3, 8)
2. Future tense (Ch. 14)
3. Adjective: *clever*; Noun described: *fox* (Ch. 6)
4. Verb of being (Ch. 8)
5. Conjunction: *but*; shows contrast (Ch. 11)
6. *an* (Ch. 12)
7. Passive (Ch. 21)

**Section 2 — Vocabulary**
1. Any reasonable pair, e.g., synonym: joyful/glad/cheerful; antonym: sad (Ch. 17)
2. Their (Ch. 18)
3. It's (Ch. 18)
4. unkind — meaning "not kind" (Ch. 19)
5. careful — e.g., "She was careful with the glass." (Ch. 19)
6. Any precise replacement, e.g., "The food was delicious." (Ch. 17, 20)

**Section 3 — Subject-Verb Agreement and Tense**
1. is (Ch. 13)
2. "The children play outside every afternoon." (Ch. 13)
3. "Yesterday, we went to the fair and we ate ice cream." (Ch. 14)
4. is barking (Ch. 15)
5. "Leo will eat seeds." (Ch. 14)
6. "Every day, Maya feeds her fish. Yesterday, she cleaned the tank." (Ch. 15)

**Section 4 — Editing**
Corrected passage: "Yesterday my family went to the beach. We saw lots of shells and my brother collected some. It was a very hot day, and everyone was tired by the evening. I loved the beach."
Eight errors: *yesterday* → capital Y (Ch. 16); *go* → went (Ch. 14); *we sees* → we saw (Ch. 13/14); *collect* → collected (Ch. 14); *its* → it was (Ch. 14/18, tense + word choice); *everyone were* → everyone was (Ch. 13); *i* → capital I (Ch. 16); *loves* → loved (Ch. 14).

**Section 5 — Writing**
Accept any paragraph with a clear topic sentence, at least two sensory details, and consistent tense throughout, per Chapter 24's evaluation criteria.

### Practice Test B

**Section 1 — Grammar Identification**
1. Subject: *The old clock*; Predicate: *struck thirteen* (Ch. 1)
2. Preposition: *at*; kind: time (Ch. 10)
3. Pronoun: *us*; object pronoun (Ch. 5)
4. Exclamation (Ch. 2)
5. Adverb: *beautifully*; describes *sang* (Ch. 9)
6. "Our homework was checked by the teacher." (Ch. 21)

**Section 2 — Vocabulary**
1. Antonym pair: happy / sad; synonym pair: joyful / cheerful; antonym pair: tiny / huge (Ch. 17)
2. two (Ch. 18)
3. deaf (Ch. 19)
4. "Careful" means full of care; "careless" means without care (Ch. 19)
5. Any reduced-repetition rewrite, e.g., "The tall boy climbed the tree to see the building." (Ch. 20)

**Section 3 — Sentence Construction and Transformation**
1. "It was raining, so we stayed inside." (Ch. 20)
2. "Maya said that she was tired." (Ch. 22)
3. Rohan said, "I am hungry." (Ch. 22)
4. Any reasonable expansion, e.g., "After school, the children laughed in the playground." (Ch. 20)
5. "The girl quickly threw the ball." (Ch. 20)

**Section 4 — Editing**
Corrected passage: "My sister and I are going to the library. We read books there every Saturday. Last week she borrowed three books and I borrowed two. It's always quiet inside and everyone whispers. I love the library."
Nine errors: *my* → capital M (Ch. 16); *me* → I, as part of the compound subject "My sister and I" (Ch. 5); *is* → are, since "My sister and I" is a plural subject joined by "and" (Ch. 13); *we reads* → we read (Ch. 13); *saturday* → capital S (Ch. 16); *borrow* → borrowed (Ch. 14); *borrows* → borrowed (Ch. 13/14); *its* → It's (Ch. 18); *whisper* → whispers (Ch. 13); *loves* → love (Ch. 13).

*(Note: this list has ten entries because "me is" is being split into two separate, clearly explainable fixes — a pronoun-case fix and an agreement fix — which together count as the ninth distinct error location in the passage.)*

**Section 5 — Writing**
Accept any letter with all five parts (address/date, greeting, body, closing, signature) per Chapter 25, or any story completion with consistent tense and at least one correctly punctuated line of dialogue per Chapter 26.

---

## 13. SELF-CHECK

I can:

- [ ] complete a full, mixed-section practice test within a set time.
- [ ] apply grammar, vocabulary, editing, and writing skills together, without new teaching support.
- [ ] mark my own or a partner's work accurately against an answer key.
- [ ] identify one or two areas I need to revise further.
- [ ] find the correct earlier chapter to revisit for any topic I am unsure about.

---

## 14. TEACHER SUPPORT NOTE

Likely misconception: Students often treat a low score on a practice test as a final judgement rather than as diagnostic information, which can discourage further revision exactly when it would help most.

Support strategy: Before returning marked practice tests, remind the class explicitly that the purpose of Section 9 ("Where to Revise") is to turn every wrong answer into a specific, findable next step — not a final grade. Consider having students set one personal revision goal (e.g., "revise Chapter 14 before Friday") rather than only recording a score.

Extension: Once both practice tests have been completed and marked, consider setting a third, teacher-authored practice test that mixes items from Tests A and B in a new order, to check whether performance holds up outside the exact format already seen.

Challenge: Ask advanced learners to write one full extra section (any of the five types used here) of their own, complete with a working answer key, for the rest of the class to attempt.

---

## 15. STATUS

`Chapter 29 has been audited against BookQualityAudit.md. Three content/answer-key errors were found and corrected: (1) an editorial artifact left inside the Practice Test A editing answer key ("— wait, check tense: —") has been removed; (2) the Practice Test B vocabulary-sorting question and its answer key were internally inconsistent and did not account for all six given words — both the question and answer key were rewritten so the six words sort cleanly into three synonym/antonym pairs; (3) the Practice Test B editing passage was stated to contain eight errors but actually contains nine — the instruction text and answer key were both corrected, and the "me is" fix was split into its two component corrections (pronoun case and agreement) for clarity.

Structural note (ratified, not a defect): this chapter deliberately departs from the Pattern C section sequence used by Chapters 14-28 (see ChapterTemplate.md Section 38, "Do Not Force the Template"). As the book's final, assessment-focused chapter, it omits Language Experience, Look and Notice, Grammar Word, Core Explanation, Types/Classifications, Worked Example, Compare and Notice, Common Confusion, Language in Real Life, Speaking/Listening, Image Placeholder, Exact Educational Graphic, Practice Zone, Real-Life Task, and LaTeX Semantic Mapping/Chapter File in their usual form, replacing them with two full practice tests (Sections 6-7), test-taking strategy guidance (Section 5), self-marking guidance (Section 8), a chapter-finder revision table (Section 9), and a single integrated Answer Key Data section (Section 12) covering both tests. This departure has been reviewed during this audit pass and is ratified as appropriate for a revision/testing chapter rather than treated as a structural gap.

REVISED.`
